T_{m}
in the statement of Lemma
4.9
,
\min\{\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(T_{m_{k}}),-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}(T_{m_{k}})\}
is strictly increasing in
k
, hence goes to
\infty
as
k\to\infty
. Therefore,
\mathbbm{P}[E_{k}]\to 1
as
k\to\infty
. Taking the limit as
k\to\infty
in (
4.14
) therefore gives
\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X^{L}X^{R}}}\right]=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X^{L}}}\right]\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X^{R}}}\right]
. Since this holds for any possible choices of
X^{L}
and
X^{R}
, we get that
\widehat{M}_{s}^{L}
and
\widehat{M}_{s}^{R}
are independent. As
s\to\infty
,
\widehat{M}_{s}^{L}
and
\widehat{M}_{s}^{R}
increase to
\widehat{M}^{L}
and
\widehat{M}^{R}
, respectively. Sending
s\to\infty
now concludes the proof.
∎

5
Existence and basic properties of the Busemann function

5.1
Setup and outline

In this section, we will prove Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
.
We consider the following setup.
Fix
{\operatorname{XDP}}\in\{\operatorname{LDP},\operatorname{SDP}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}):\mathbbm{Z}^{2}\to\mathbbm{Z}
be the encoding walk for the UIBOT
(M_{-\infty,\infty},\lambda_{0})
as in Proposition
3.10
. For
n\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let

\lambda_{n}=(\lambda^{-}_{n},\lambda^{+}_{n})

be the active edge of the KMSW procedure at time
n
, with the indexing chosen so that
\lambda_{0}
is the root edge.

For
n\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let
M_{n,\infty}
be the infinite acyclic-oriented triangulation with missing edges obtained by applying the KMSW procedure to
(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+n)-\mathcal{Z}(n))|_{[n,\infty)}
. By Remark
3.4
,
M_{-\infty,\infty}
can be recovered from
(M_{n,\infty})_{n\in\mathbbm{Z}}
by taking their union. Boundary edges to the west of
\lambda_{n}
are not part of
M_{n,\infty}
, i.e. are missing edges.

Let
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
be the submap of
M_{n,\infty}
induced by the vertices of
M_{n,\infty}
which are reachable by a directed path in
M_{n,\infty}
started from the initial vertex
\lambda^{-}_{n}
. That is,
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
consists of this vertex set, all of the edges of
M_{n,\infty}
whose vertices are both in this vertex set, and all of the faces of
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
whose three boundary vertices are all in this vertex set.

The left and right boundaries of
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
are the leftmost and rightmost directed paths in
M_{n,\infty}
from
\lambda^{-}_{n}
to
\infty
(note that these two paths are
not
disjoint in general). In particular,
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
has no missing edges.
The rightmost directed path in
M_{n,\infty}
from
\lambda^{-}_{n}
to
\infty
is just the set of non-missing boundary edges of
M_{n,\infty}
, i.e. all the boundary edges to the east of
\lambda_{n}
, including
\lambda_{n}
itself.
We denote the leftmost directed path from
\lambda^{-}_{n}
to
\infty
in
M_{n,\infty}
by

\beta_{n}:\mathbbm{N}\to\mathcal{E}(M_{n,\infty}),

(5.1)

noting that such a path exists thanks to the final claim in Lemma
3.8
.
By definition, all of the edges of
\beta_{n}
are included in
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}^{\prime}
be the submap of
M_{n,\infty}
obtained by removing from
M_{n,\infty}
all of the vertices, edges, and faces of
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
(including the ones on
\beta_{n}
). Then
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}^{\prime}
is a directed triangulation with missing edges (every boundary edge of
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}^{\prime}
is a missing edge). See Figure
9
for an illustration of the above definitions.

Figure 9:

Illustration of the definitions of the maps
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
,
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}^{\prime}
obtained from
M_{n,\infty}
and
\beta_{n}
, in the case when
n=0
. The edges on
\beta_{0}
belong to
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
but not to
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
.

The following proposition makes the connection between the results of Section
4
and the UIBOT.

Proposition 5.1
.

For each
n\in\mathbbm{Z}
, the maps
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}^{\prime}
are independent. Furthermore, the map
\widehat{M}_{n,\infty}
has the same law as the UIQBOT
\widehat{M}
of Definition
4.1
.

We will prove Proposition
5.1
in Section
5.5
using the KMSW bijection.

As in Theorem
1.4
, we let
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be the boundary vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
, enumerated in directed order in such a way that
x_{0}=\lambda_{0}^{-}
and
x_{1}=\lambda_{0}^{+}
are the initial and terminal vertices of
\lambda_{0}
, respectively.
For
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let

\tau_{k}:=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\in\mathbbm{N}_{0}:\text{$x_{k}$ is the initial vertex of $\lambda_{n}$}}}\right\}.

(5.2)

Recalling Definition
3.2
, it is easy to see from the Assertions
(i)
and
(ii)
of Lemma
3.6
(note that in Assertions
(i)
we are counting in the reversed order compared to how boundary vertices are enumerated in
M_{0,\infty}
; this is why we have
\mathcal{L}(n)=k
instead of
\mathcal{L}(n)=-k
in the last case of (
5.3
)) that

\tau_{k}=\begin{cases}0,\quad&k=0\\
\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\in\mathbbm{N}:\mathcal{R}(n)=-k}}\right\},\quad&k\geq 1\\
\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\in\mathbbm{N}:\mathcal{L}(n)=k}}\right\},\quad&k\leq-1.\end{cases}

(5.3)

In particular,
\tau_{k}
is increasing for
k\geq 0
and decreasing for
k\leq 0
.

In Section
5.2
, we will prove Theorem
1.4
using the existence of cut vertices in the UIQBOT (Proposition
4.3
). More precisely, we will force long directed paths started from boundary vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
to pass through the cut vertices of one of the maps
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
(which has the law of the UIQBOT by Proposition
5.1
and the strong Markov property of
\mathcal{Z}
).

In Section
5.3
, we will use the left and right independence property of Proposition
4.7
(applied to the UIQBOTs
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k}}
), together with the strong Markov property of
\mathcal{Z}
, to prove the independent and stationary increments properties in Theorem
1.7
(Properties
(i)
and
(ii)
). Property
(iii)
follows from elementary monotonicity considerations.

What remains is then to prove Property
(iv)
. To do this, in Section
5.4
we introduce a new infinite acyclically-oriented triangulation with boundary, which we call the uniform infinite boundary-channeled half-plane bipolar-oriented triangulation (UIBHBOT; Definition
5.8
). The UIBHBOT describes the local limit of uniformly sampled finite boundary-channeled triangulations (Definition
3.17
) around a uniform boundary edge (Proposition
5.16
). Moreover, the UIBHBOT admits submaps which have the same law as
M_{0,\infty}
(Lemma
5.10
). Using the symmetry in Lemma
3.18
, one can show that the law of the UIBHBOT is invariant under applying an orientation-reversing homeomorphism from
\mathbbm{C}\to\mathbbm{C}
(Proposition
5.9
). Combining these last two facts leads to Property
(iv)
.

5.2
Existence of the Busemann function

Figure 10:

Illustration of the proof of Lemma
5.2
in the case when
k\geq 1
(left) and
k\leq-1
(right).

Lemma 5.2
.

Define
\tau_{k}
as in (
5.2
).
For each
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, the map
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
has the same law as the map
M_{0,\infty}
and the map
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
has the same law as the UIQBOT (Definition
4.1
). Furthermore, every directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{k}
is contained in the map
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
defined just above (
5.1
).

Proof.

See Figure
10
for an illustration of the proof.
By (
5.3
), each
\tau_{k}
is a stopping time for
\mathcal{Z}
.
By the strong Markov property, the map
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
(which is determined by
(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+\tau_{k})-\mathcal{Z}(\tau_{k}))|_{[0,\infty)}
) has the same law as
M_{0,\infty}
. By this and Proposition
5.1
,
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
has the law of the UIQBOT.

It remains to prove the statement about directed paths. This statement holds by definition for
k=0
, so we only need to prove it for
k\not=0
. We first argue that for
k\in\mathbbm{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, every directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{k}
is contained in
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
. Let
P
be such a directed path.

First, consider the case when
k\geq 1
.
Let
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{L}
be the path formed by the missing edges on the boundary of
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
lying to the west of
\lambda_{\tau_{k}}^{-}=x_{k}
.
By Lemma
3.8
,
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{L}
is the leftmost directed path in
M_{-\infty,\infty}
ending at
x_{k}
and every vertex on
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{L}
has no incoming edges in
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
. Since
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{L}
is the leftmost directed path ending at
x_{k}
, all of the edges of
M_{0,\infty}\setminus M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
incident to
x_{k}
must be oriented toward
x_{k}
. Therefore, the first step of
P
must be from
x_{k}
to a vertex of
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
, and
P
cannot visit any vertex on
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{L}
after its first step. The path
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{L}
disconnects
M_{0,\infty}\setminus M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
from
\infty
in
M_{0,\infty}
. Hence
P
cannot enter
M_{0,\infty}\setminus M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
, so
P
must be contained in
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
.

Next, consider the case when
k\leq-1
. Let
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
be the path formed by the edges in the boundary of
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
lying to the east of the terminal vertex
\lambda_{\tau_{k}}^{+}
of
\lambda_{\tau_{k}}
(see the dark blue path on the right-hand side of Figure
10
). By Lemma
3.8
,
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
is the rightmost directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
\lambda^{+}_{\tau_{k}}
. It follows that
P
cannot cross
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
: indeed, otherwise concatenating part of
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
with the portion of
P
lying to the right of
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
would yield a directed path started from
\lambda^{+}_{\tau_{k}}
which lies partially to the right of
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
.

By (
5.2
),
\tau_{k}
is the
first
non-negative time at which
x_{k}
is the initial vertex of
\lambda_{n}
, and
M_{0,\infty}
does not contain the edges along its left boundary since they are missing.
Consequently, there are no non-missing edges of
M_{0,\tau_{k}-1}=M_{0,\infty}\setminus M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
incident to
x_{k}
.
In particular, the first edge of
P
is not in
M_{0,\tau_{k}-1}
. Since
P
is a directed path and the orientation on
M_{0,\infty}
is acyclic,
P
can visit
x_{k}
only once. It follows that
P
cannot visit a edge of
M_{0,\tau_{k}-1}
without crossing
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
. We already saw that
P
cannot cross
\beta_{\tau_{k}}^{R}
, so
P
cannot visit a edge of
M_{0,\tau_{k}-1}
.

Recall that
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
is the submap of
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
induced by the set of vertices of
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
reachable by a directed path in
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
started from
x_{k}
. Since every such directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{k}
is contained in
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
, any such directed path is in fact contained in
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
.
∎

The following lemma tells us that any finite collection of sufficiently long directed paths started from vertices on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
must have a vertex in common. This will be the key tool for the proof of the existence of the Busemann function (Theorem
1.4
).

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be the sequence of boundary vertices as in Theorem
1.4
.
For each
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{Z}
with
k<k^{\prime}
, there exists a sequence of distinct vertices
\{v_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}
of
M_{0,\infty}
with the following property. Let
l\in[k,k^{\prime}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
and let
\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbbm{N}}
be any sequence of distinct vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
which can each be reached by a directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{l}
. Then for each
m\in\mathbbm{N}
, it holds for each sufficiently large
j\in\mathbbm{N}
that every directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{l}
to
w_{j}
visits
v_{m}
.

Proof.

The proof is based on the fact that the UIQBOT has infinitely many cut vertices (Proposition
4.3
).
Let
\tau_{k}
be as in (
5.2
).
By Lemma
5.2
, the map
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
has the same law as the UIQBOT.

Let
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{Z}
with
k<k^{\prime}
.
Since the KMSW procedure only explores finitely many edges between times
\tau_{k}
and
\tau_{k^{\prime}}
, the right boundaries of the maps
M_{\tau_{l},\infty}
for
l\in[k,k^{\prime}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
(equivalently, the right boundaries of the maps
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{l},\infty}
for
l\in[k,k^{\prime}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
) coincide except for finitely many edges.
In particular, there is an infinite directed path
\beta_{\tau_{k},\tau_{k^{\prime}}}^{R}
which is part of the right boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{l},\infty}
for each
l\in[k,k^{\prime}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
. Almost surely, the right boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
has infinitely many cut vertices (Proposition
4.3
). So, a.s. there are infinitely many cut vertices of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
which lie on
\beta_{\tau_{k},\tau_{k^{\prime}}}^{R}
. Let
\{v_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}
be the ordered sequence of these cut vertices.

For
l\in[k,k^{\prime}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
, the leftmost directed path
\beta_{\tau_{l}}
of (
5.1
) (which is the left boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{l},\infty}
) cannot cross
\beta_{\tau_{k}}
(which is the left boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
). Hence every cut vertex of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
which lies on
\beta_{\tau_{k},\tau_{k^{\prime}}}^{R}
(i.e., each of the vertices
v_{m}
) is also a cut vertex of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{l},\infty}
.
By Lemma
5.2
, every directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{l}
must be contained in
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{l},\infty}
. For each
m\in\mathbbm{N}
, there are only finitely many vertices of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{l},\infty}
which are not disconnected from
x_{l}
when we remove the cut vertex
v_{m}
. It follows that there are only finitely many vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
which are reachable by a directed path started from
x_{l}
in
M_{0,\infty}
which does not visit
v_{m}
. If
\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbbm{N}}
is as in the lemma statement, then if
j
is sufficiently large,
w_{j}
is not equal to any of these finitely many vertices, for any
l\in[k,k^{\prime}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
. This implies the lemma statement.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Let
{\operatorname{XDP}}\in\{\operatorname{LDP},\operatorname{SDP}\}
and
K\in\mathbbm{N}
. By Lemma
5.3
, there exists a vertex
z=z(K)
of
M_{0,\infty}
with the following property. Let
k\in[-K,K]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
and let
\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbbm{N}}
be a sequence of distinct vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
which can each be reached from
x_{k}
by a directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
.
Then, it holds for each sufficiently large
j\in\mathbbm{N}
that every directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{k}
to
w_{j}
visits
z
. Indeed, we can take
z
to be one of the vertices
v_{m}
in Lemma
5.3
(with
-K
and
K
in place of
k
and
k^{\prime}
).
We define

\mathcal{X}(k):={\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},z)-{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},z),\quad\forall k\in[-K,K]\cap\mathbbm{Z}.

(5.4)

We claim that the function defined in (
5.4
) satisfies the defining property (
1.2
) from the theorem statement for all
k,k^{\prime}\in[-K,K]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
.
Indeed, let
\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be a (new) sequence of distinct vertices which can be reached by a directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{k}
or started from
x_{k^{\prime}}

Then for each sufficiently large
j
, each
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{k}
to
w_{j}
visits
z
, which implies that

{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{j})={\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},z)+{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(z,w_{j})

The analogous relation also holds with
k^{\prime}
in place of
k
. By subtracting these two relations, we obtain that for each sufficiently large
j
,

\mathcal{X}(k^{\prime})-\mathcal{X}(k)={\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k^{\prime}},w_{j})-{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{j}),

which is (
1.2
).

It is clear that there is a at most one function satisfying
\mathcal{X}(0)=0
and (
1.2
) with
k,k^{\prime}
restricted to lie in
[-K,K]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
. Since we have constructed such a function on
[-K,K]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
for an arbitrary
K\in\mathbbm{N}
, we have proven the theorem statement.
∎

5.3
Independent and stationary increments

In this section, we will prove Properties
(i)
through
(iii)
of Theorem
1.7
. We first observe that leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesics to
\infty
in
M_{0,\infty}
(Definition
4.5
) exist and satisfy a coalescence property.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be the ordered sequence of boundary vertices as in Theorem
1.4
.
Almost surely, for each
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, there exists a (unique) leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic
P_{x_{k}}
from
x_{k}
to
\infty
in
M_{0,\infty}
. These leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesics coalesce, in the sense that for any
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{Z}
, there exists
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{N}
such that
P_{x_{k}}(t+\theta)=P_{x_{k^{\prime}}}(t+\theta^{\prime})
for each
t\in\mathbbm{N}
.

Proof.

Define
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
as in (
5.2
).
Since
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
has the law of the UIQBOT (Lemma
5.2
), Lemma
4.6
implies that there is a unique leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic
P_{x_{k}}
from
x_{k}
to
\infty
in
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
. Since every directed path from
x_{k}
to
\infty
in
M_{0,\infty}
is contained in
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k}}
(Lemma
5.2
), the path
P_{x_{k}}
is in fact the unique leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic from
x_{k}
to
\infty
in
M_{0,\infty}
.

By Lemma
5.3
, if we define the sequence of vertices
\{v_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}
for
x_{k}
and
x_{k^{\prime}}
as in that lemma, then each of
P_{x_{k}}
and
P_{x_{k^{\prime}}}
must visit
v_{m}
for every
m\in\mathbbm{N}
.
In particular,
P_{x_{k}}
and
P_{x_{k^{\prime}}}
have at least one vertex in common.
By the uniqueness of leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesics,
P_{x_{k}}
and
P_{x_{k^{\prime}}}
coincide after the first time they meet.
This gives the coalescence property.
∎

The following lemma is the main input in the proofs of the independent and stationary increments properties in Theorem
1.7
(Properties
(i)
and
(ii)
).
It is a consequence of Proposition
4.7
.

Figure 11:

Illustration of the submaps used in the proof of Lemma
5.5
in the case when
k\geq 0
(left) and the case when
k\leq 0
(right).

Lemma 5.5
.

For
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}
(resp.
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{R}
) be the submap of
M_{0,\infty}
lying to the left (resp. right) of the leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic
P_{x_{k}}
of Lemma
5.4
, including the vertices and edges lying on
P_{x_{k}}
.
Then
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}
and
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{R}
are independent. Furthermore, if
k\geq 0
then
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{R}\overset{d}{=}\mathring{M}_{x_{0},\infty}^{R}
and if
k\leq 0
then
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}\overset{d}{=}\mathring{M}_{x_{0},\infty}^{L}
.

Proof.

See Figure
11
for an illustration.

Define
\tau_{k}
as in (
5.2
). Let
M_{0,\tau_{k}}
be the submap of
M_{0,\infty}
obtained by applying the KMSW procedure to
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\tau_{k}]}
(Definition
3.3
). By (
5.3
), each
\tau_{k}
is a stopping time for
\mathcal{Z}
. Since
M_{0,\tau_{k}}
is determined by
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\tau_{k}]}
and
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
is determined by
(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+\tau_{k})-\mathcal{Z}(\tau_{k}))|_{[0,\infty)}
, the maps
M_{0,\tau_{k}}
and
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
are independent.

Define the maps
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime}
as in the discussion just above Proposition
5.1
. By Proposition
5.1
and Lemma
5.2
, the maps
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime}
are independent and their joint law does not depend on
k
. Since the maps
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime}
are a.s. determined by
M_{\tau_{k},\infty}
, combining this with the previous paragraph shows that the triple
(M_{0,\tau_{k}},\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty},\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime})
is independent.

By Lemma
5.2
,
P_{x_{k}}
is the leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic from
x_{k}
to
\infty
in
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
.
By Lemma
5.2
, the map
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
has the same law as the UIQBOT.
Hence, Proposition
4.7
implies the following. Let
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L}
(resp.
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}
) be the submap of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}
consisting of the vertices, edges, and faces lying to the left (resp. right) of
P_{x_{k}}
, including the vertices and edges lying on
P_{x_{k}}
. Then
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L}
and
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}
are independent. By combining this with the previous paragraph, we get that the 4-tuple
(M_{0,\tau_{k}},\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L},\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R},\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime})
is independent. Moreover, the laws of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L},\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}
, and
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime}
do not depend on
k
.

When
k\geq 0
, the map
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{R}
is equal to
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}
. The map
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}
is obtained by removing the edge
(x_{k},x_{k+1})
from
M_{0,\tau_{k}}
to get
M_{0,\tau_{k}-1}
, then identifying the maps
M_{0,\tau_{k}-1}
,
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L}
, and
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime}
together along their boundaries. In particular, the upper boundary of
M_{0,\tau_{k}-1}
(Definition
3.3
) is identified with a segment of the left boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime}
starting from the vertex
x_{k}
, and the right boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{\prime}
is identified with the left boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L}
. By the independence statement from the previous paragraph and the fact that the law of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}
does not depend on
k
, we get the lemma statement in the case when
k\geq 0
.

In the case when
k\leq 0
, the map
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}
is obtained by identifying the right boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k}}^{\prime}
with the left boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L}
. The map
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{R}
is obtained by identifying the upper boundary of
M_{0,\tau_{k}}
with a segment of the right boundary of
\widehat{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}
started at
x_{k}
. Thus the lemma statement similarly follows in this case.
∎

For
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let
P_{x_{k}}
be the leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic from
x_{k}
to
\infty
in
M_{0,\infty}
, as in Lemma
5.4
.
We will now use Lemma
5.5
to decompose
M_{0,\infty}
into countably many independent “geodesic slices” lying between successive leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesics
P_{x_{k-1}}
and
P_{x_{k}}
. For
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let
\theta_{k}^{-},\theta_{k}\in\mathbbm{N}_{0}
be the smallest numbers such that

P_{x_{k-1}}(t+\theta_{k}^{-})=P_{x_{k}}(t+\theta_{k}),\quad\forall t\in\mathbbm{N}.

(5.5)

Such numbers exist by Lemma
5.4
.

Figure 12:

Illustration of the
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic slices
G_{k}
defined in Lemma
5.6
(regions between the orange paths). For
k\geq 0
,
G_{k}
can consist of a single edge (which means that
\theta_{k}^{-}=1
and
\theta_{k}=0
) as shown for
G_{4}
, but for
k\leq-1
this is not possible since
M_{0,\infty}
does not include the boundary edges to the left of
x_{0}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
G_{k}
be the (directed) submap of
M_{0,\infty}
consisting of the vertices, edges, and faces of
M_{0,\infty}
which lie between
P_{x_{k-1}}([1,\theta_{k}^{-}])
and
P_{x_{k}}([1,\theta_{k}])
, including the vertices and edges which lie on these
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic segments. We call
G_{k}
an
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesic slice
(see Figure
12
). The maps
\{G_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
are independent. Furthermore, the maps
\{G_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
all have the same law, and the maps
\{G_{k}\}_{k\leq 0}
all have the same law.

Proof.

Define the maps
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}
and
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{R}
as in Lemma
5.5
. Since leftmost
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesics cannot cross each other, for
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, the maps
\{G_{j}\}_{j\leq k}
are a.s. determined by
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}
and the maps
\{G_{j}\}_{j\geq k+1}
are a.s. determined by
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{R}
. By the independence part of Lemma
5.5
, the sequence of maps
\{G_{j}\}_{j\leq k}
is independent from the sequence of maps
\{G_{j}\}_{j\geq k+1}
. Since this holds for every
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, the sequence of maps
\{G_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
are independent from each other.
To get the stationarity property, we observe that
G_{k}
is determined by each of
\mathring{M}_{x_{k},\infty}^{L}
and
\mathring{M}_{x_{k-1},\infty}^{R}
in a manner which does not depend on
k
. The stationarity property for
G_{k}
therefore follows from the stationarity part of Lemma
5.5
.
∎

The Busemann function can be expressed in terms of the geodesic slices
G_{k}
as follows.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\theta_{k}^{-}
and
\theta_{k}
be as in (
5.5
). Then the Busemann function (Theorem
1.4
) satisfies

\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)=\theta_{k}-\theta_{k}^{-}.

(5.6)

Proof.

Let
w_{0}
be the terminal vertex of
P_{x_{k}}(\theta_{k})
, equivalently, the terminal vertex of
P_{x_{k-1}}(\theta_{k}^{-})
.
Let
\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbbm{N}}
be the ordered sequence of vertices visited by
P_{x_{k}}
(equivalently,
P_{x_{k-1}}
) after
w_{0}
.
Since
P_{x_{k}}
and
P_{x_{k-1}}
are
{\operatorname{XDP}}
geodesics, for each
j\in\mathbbm{N}
,

{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{j})={\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{0})+{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(w_{0},w_{j})

and the same is true with
x_{k-1}
in place of
x_{k}
. Therefore,

{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{j})-{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k-1},w_{j})={\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{0})-{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k-1},w_{0})=\theta_{k}-\theta_{k}^{-}.

By the definition of
\mathcal{X}
from Theorem
1.4
, this implies (
5.6
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
, Properties
(i)
through
(iii)
.

By Lemma
5.7
, the increment
\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)
is a function of the XDP geodesic slice map
G_{k}
of Lemma
5.6
. Therefore, the independent increments and stationary increments properties
(i)
and
(ii)
follow from Lemma
5.6
.
To get Property
(iii)
, let
k\geq 1
. Use Theorem
1.4
to find
w\in M_{0,\infty}
such that

\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)={\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w)-{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k-1},w).

(5.7)

Since the directed edge
(x_{k-1},x_{k})
is part of
M_{0,\infty}
, Property
(iii)
is immediate from (
5.7
).
∎

5.4
Symmetry via the UIBHBOT

To prove Theorem
1.7
, it remains to prove Property
(iv)
.
For this purpose, we will introduce an auxiliary infinite directed planar map, which we now define.
Let
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}=(\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{b}},\mathcal{R}^{\operatorname{b}}):\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
be a bi-infinite walk such that
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(0)=(0,0)
;
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}|_{[0,\infty)}\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\infty)}
; and

\text{$\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}|_{(-\infty,0]}$ has the law of $\mathcal{Z}|_{(-\infty,0]}$ conditioned so that $\mathcal{L}(n)\geq 1$ for every $n\leq-1$}.

To be concrete, one way to construct
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}|_{(-\infty,0]}
is as follows. Let
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{k}(\cdot)\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be an i.i.d. collection of random walk paths such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{k}
has the same law as
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\tau_{-1}]}
, where
\tau_{-1}=\min\{n\in\mathbbm{N}:\mathcal{L}(n)=-1\}
(as in (
5.3
)). Let
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}:\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
be obtained by concatenating the paths
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{k}
end-to-end in order, in such a way that
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(0)=(0,0)
and 0 is the time at which we start traversing the path
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
. Then for
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, the path
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{k}
is the segment of
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
between the first time that
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{b}}
hits
-k
and the first time that
\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{b}}
hits
-k-1
.
It follows from the strong Markov property that
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}|_{[0,\infty)}\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\infty)}
. It can be seen from Lemma
5.14
below that
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}|_{(-\infty,0]}
has the same law as the process obtained from
\mathcal{Z}|_{(-\infty,0]}
by conditioning
\mathcal{L}|_{(-\infty,1]}
to stay positive in the sense of
[
BD94
]
(but we will not need to use this latter fact).

By Definition
3.3
and Remark
3.4
, we can apply the KMSW procedure to
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
to produce an infinite bipolar-oriented triangulation
M^{\operatorname{b}}
. As we will explain just below,
M^{\operatorname{b}}
has an infinite boundary, and all of its boundary edges are missing (i.e. it only has an infinite lower-left boundary); see Figure
13
for an illustration of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
.

We describe
M^{\operatorname{b}}
more precisely.
For
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let

\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}:=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\in\mathbbm{Z}:\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{b}}(n)=k}}\right\}.

(5.8)

By the definition of
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
,
\{\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
is a decreasing sequence of times and
\tau_{0}^{\operatorname{b}}=0
.
By Assertion
(i)
of Lemma
3.6
, for each
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, the time
\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}
is the time when the triangle of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
whose boundary includes the
k
th lower-left (missing) boundary edge of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
is added to
M^{\operatorname{b}}
by the KMSW procedure, with the lower-left boundary edges enumerated from west to east. Hence, the (missing) boundary edges are added from east to west by the KMSW procedure. Since, a.s.,

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{b}}(n)=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{R}^{\operatorname{b}}(n)=\liminf_{n\to-\infty}\mathcal{R}^{\operatorname{b}}(n)=-\infty,

Lemma
3.6
and Remark
3.4
imply that a.s. all of the boundary edges of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
belong to the lower-left boundary.

Let
\{x^{\operatorname{b}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be the vertices along the (lower-left) boundary of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
, enumerated so that
(x^{\operatorname{b}}_{k},x^{\operatorname{b}}_{k+1})
is the edge corresponding to time
\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}
. Since, as explained above, the edges
\{(x^{\operatorname{b}}_{k},x^{\operatorname{b}}_{k+1})\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
are enumerated from west to east, the vertices
\{x_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
are also enumerated from west to east.
We write
e^{\operatorname{b}}=(x^{\operatorname{b}}_{0},x^{\operatorname{b}}_{1})
and call it the
root edge
of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
. Note that here we are not respecting the usual convention that maps are rooted at
\lambda_{0}
, the reason being that
\lambda_{0}
is not a boundary edge for
M^{\operatorname{b}}
(rather, its initial vertex is
x^{\operatorname{b}}_{0}
and its terminal vertex is not on the boundary).
Also note that
e^{\operatorname{b}}
is a missing edge of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
.

Definition 5.8
.

The
uniform infinite boundary-channeled half-plane bipolar-oriented triangulation
(UIBHBOT) is the edge-rooted triangulation with boundary
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
defined just above.

Figure 13:

The UIBHBOT
M^{\operatorname{b}}
and the submap
M^{\operatorname{b}}_{\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{0,\infty}
for a typical value of
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
. The KMSW procedure first explores the yellow region and then the grey region. The triangles containing a boundary edge are explored in east to west order. Also shown is the active edge for the KMSW procedure at time
\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}
with the corresponding triangle having a (missing) boundary edge. Note that all the boundary edges of the UIBHBOT are missing. By, e.g., Proposition
5.16
below, each of the boundary vertices of the UIBHBOT has no incoming edges.

The reason for the name is that the UIHBHBOT arises as the Benjamini-Schramm local limit of certain uniform boundary-channeled bipolar-oriented triangulations, as defined in Definition
3.17
, around a uniformly chosen boundary edge (see Proposition
5.16
below).
For the purposes of proving Theorem
1.7
, the main fact about the UIBHBOT which we need is the following non-trivial symmetry.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
be the UIBHBOT.
Let
(\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}^{\operatorname{b}})
be the directed, edge-rooted triangulation with missing boundary edges obtained from
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
by applying an orientation-reversing homeomorphism
\mathbbm{C}\to\mathbbm{C}
.
Then
(\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}^{\operatorname{b}})
has the same law as
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
.

Recall that a planar map is viewed modulo
orientation-preserving
homeomorphisms from
\mathbbm{C}
to
\mathbbm{C}
.
Applying an orientation-reversing homeomorphism to
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
gives us a different planar map.
Proposition
5.9
tells us that this new planar map has the same law as
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
.

In the setting of Proposition
5.9
, each (non-missing) edge of
\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}}
is equipped with the same orientation as the corresponding edge of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
(it is only the orientation of the plane which is reversed, not the orientation of the edges of the map).

Proposition
5.9
is not obvious from the definition of the UIBHBOT in terms of the KMSW procedure.
Indeed, the KMSW encoding walk corresponding to
(\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}^{\operatorname{b}})
is coupled with the original KMSW encoding walk
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
in a highly non-trivial way.
Reversing the orientation of
\mathbbm{C}
swaps west and east, so if
\{x^{\operatorname{b}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
is the west to east ordered sequence of boundary vertices of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
, as in the discussion just above (
5.8
), then
\{x^{\operatorname{b}}_{-k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
is the west to east ordered sequence of boundary vertices of
\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}}
.

We will prove Proposition
5.9
in Section
5.6
using a symmetry for finite boundary-channeled bipolar-oriented triangulations (Lemma
3.18
) and a limiting argument. For the time being, we use Proposition
5.9
to conclude the proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
be the UIBHBOT (Definition
5.8
).
For
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
, let
\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}
be as in (
5.8
) and let
\{x_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be the sequence of boundary vertices as in the discussion just after (
5.8
).
Let
M^{\operatorname{b}}_{\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}
be the submap of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
obtained by applying the KMSW procedure to
(\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(\cdot+\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}})-\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}))|_{[0,\infty)}
.
Then
M_{\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{0,\infty}
.
Furthermore, for each
l\leq k
, each directed path in
M^{\operatorname{b}}
from
x_{l}^{\operatorname{b}}
to
\infty
is contained in
M_{\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}
.

Proof.

In the construction of
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
described at the beginning of Section
5.4
, the process
(\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(\cdot+\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}})-\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}))|_{[0,\infty)}
is obtained by concatenating the walk paths
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{j}
for
j\geq-k
. By the strong Markov property of
\mathcal{Z}
, this implies that

(\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(\cdot+\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}})-\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}))|_{[0,\infty)}\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\infty)}
. Hence
M^{\operatorname{b}}_{\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{0,\infty}
.
The statement that each directed path in
M^{\operatorname{b}}
from
x_{l}^{\operatorname{b}}
to
\infty
is contained in
M_{\tau_{k}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}
follows from a similar argument as in the
k\leq-1
case of Lemma
5.2
.
∎

From Lemma
5.10
, we can obtain the analog of Theorem
1.4
for the UIBHBOT.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
\{x_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be the boundary vertices of the UIBHBOT as in the discussion just after (
5.8
). There exists a unique function
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}:\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}
, called the
Busemann function
, such that
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(0)=0
and the following is true. Let
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{Z}
and let
\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbbm{N}}
be a sequence of distinct vertices of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
such that for each
j\in\mathbbm{N}
, each of
x_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}
and
x_{k^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{b}}
can be joined to
w_{j}
by a directed path in
M^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then

\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k^{\prime})-\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k)={\operatorname{XDP}}_{M^{\operatorname{b}}}(x_{k^{\prime}},w_{j})-{\operatorname{XDP}}_{M^{\operatorname{b}}}(x_{k},w_{j}),\quad\text{for each sufficiently large $j\in\mathbbm{N}$.}

(5.9)

Furthermore, the increments
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k-1)
are i.i.d., and if
\mathcal{X}
is the Busemann function for
M_{0,\infty}
as in Theorem
1.4
, then

\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k-1)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1).

(5.10)

Proof.

For
K\in\mathbbm{Z}
, define the time
\tau_{K}^{\operatorname{b}}
and the map
M_{\tau_{K}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}^{\operatorname{b}}
as in Lemma
5.10
. By Lemma
5.10
,
M^{\operatorname{b}}_{\tau_{K}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{0,\infty}
and for
k\leq K
, each directed path in
M^{\operatorname{b}}
from
x_{k}^{\operatorname{b}}
to
\infty
is contained in
M^{\operatorname{b}}_{\tau_{K}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}
.
By applying Theorem
1.4
to
M^{\operatorname{b}}_{\tau_{K}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}
, we find that there exists a unique
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}:(-\infty,K]\cap\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}
satisfying (
5.9
) for
k,k^{\prime}\leq K
.
Furthermore, by applying Properties
(i)
and
(ii)
of Theorem
1.7
(which we have already proven) to
M^{\operatorname{b}}_{\tau_{K}^{\operatorname{b}},\infty}
, we find that the increments
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k-1)
for
k\leq K
are i.i.d. and have the same law as the increment
\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)
. Since
K
can be made arbitrarily large, this proves the proposition.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
, Property
(iv)
.

Let
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
be the UIBHBOT and let
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}
be its Busemann function as in Proposition
5.11
.
By Proposition
5.9
, the law of
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
is invariant under applying an orientation-reversing homeomorphism from
\mathbbm{C}
to
\mathbbm{C}
(while keeping the directions of the edges fixed). Applying such an orientation-reversing homeomorphism has the effect of reversing the order of the boundary vertices of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
, which by (
5.9
) has the effect of replacing
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}
by its time reversal
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(-\cdot)
. Consequently, the increments of
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}
and its time reversal have the same law, i.e.,
\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k-1)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k-1)-\mathcal{X}^{\operatorname{b}}(k)
. By (
5.10
), this implies that
\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}(-1)-\mathcal{X}(0)
. Since the negative-time increments of
\mathcal{X}
all have the same law (Property
(ii)
), this implies Property
(iv)
.
∎

5.5
Proof of Proposition
5.1
: Relation between the UIBOT and the UIQBOT

The idea of the proof of Proposition
5.1
is to look at the excursions that the KMSW exploration
\lambda_{n}
makes to the left and right of the leftmost directed path
\beta_{0}
of (
5.1
). In Lemma
5.12
, we give a description in terms of the KMSW encoding walk
\mathcal{Z}
of the times
n
when
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and times
n
when
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
(recall the definitions of
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
from the beginning of Section
5.1
). We then concatenate the increments of
\mathcal{Z}
corresponding to times when
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
(resp.
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
) to obtain two KMSW encoding walks
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
for
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
, respectively (Lemma
5.13
). In Lemma
5.14
, we describe the joint law of
(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}},\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime})
, with the help of an elementary lemma about lazy random walks (Lemma
5.15
). This description immediately yields Proposition
5.1
.

We will often use in this section the following two aspects of the definitions of
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}^{\prime}_{0,\infty}
:
(i)

\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
is the submap of
M_{0,\infty}
induced by the vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
which are reachable by a directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from the initial vertex
\lambda^{-}_{0}
;
(ii)
the edges of the leftmost directed path
\beta_{0}
of (
5.1
) are non-missing edges in
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and missing edges in
\widehat{M}^{\prime}_{0,\infty}
.

Figure 14:

The times
N_{k}^{R},N_{k}^{L},
and
N_{k+1}^{R}
appearing in Lemma
5.12
and the active edges at these times. The KMSW procedure explores the light brown region, then the light blue region, then the light green region, and then the gray region.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
N_{1}^{R}=0
. Inductively, for
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
N_{k}^{L}
be the smallest
n\geq N_{k}^{R}+1
for which
\lambda_{n}\notin\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
(i.e.,
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
) and let
N_{k+1}^{R}
be the smallest
n\geq N_{k}^{L}+1
for which
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
. Then

\displaystyle N_{k}^{L}

\displaystyle=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\geq N_{k}^{R}+1:\mathcal{L}(n)=\mathcal{L}(N_{k}^{R})-1}}\right\}

\displaystyle N_{k+1}^{R}

\displaystyle=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\geq N_{k}^{L}+1:\mathcal{R}(n)=\mathcal{R}(N_{k}^{L})-1}}\right\}.

(5.11)

In particular:

(i)

At each of the times
N_{k}^{R}
for
k\geq 2
,

\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R}-1)=(1,-1),

(5.12)

and the KMSW procedure for
M_{0,\infty}
adds at time
N_{k}^{R}
a new single non-missing edge (rather than a triangular face) to
M_{0,N_{k}^{R}-1}
. This edge is
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}}
.

(ii)

At each of the times
N_{k}^{L}
for
k\geq 1
,

\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}-1)=(-1,0),

(5.13)

and the KMSW procedure for
M_{0,\infty}
creates a triangular face
T
contained in
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
whose boundary consists of the edge
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}-1}
, the (new active) edge
\lambda_{N_{k}^{L}}
, and the (previously active) edge
\lambda_{N_{k}^{L}-1}
along the path
\beta_{0}
.

Proof.

The lemma is essentially a re-statement of
[
GHS16
, Lemma 2.1]
, but
[
GHS16
]
has a slightly different setup as compared to this paper, so we will give a proof. Our proof is based on the forward KMSW bijection, in contrast to
[
GHS16
]
which uses the reverse KMSW bijection.
See Figure
14
for an illustration.

We proceed by induction on
k
. Assume that
k\in\mathbbm{N}
and the claimed description of the time
N_{k}^{R}
has been proven.
Let
x
be the vertex on the boundary of
M_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
lying immediately to the west of the initial vertex
\lambda^{-}_{N_{k}^{R}}
of
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}}
. We claim that
x\notin\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
.
For
k=1
, in which case
N_{1}^{R}=0
, the claim follows from the fact that
\beta_{0}
cannot hit the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
strictly to the west of its initial vertex
\lambda^{-}_{0}
, which in turn follows from the fact that vertices on the left boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
have no incoming edges in
M_{0,\infty}
(Lemma
3.8
).

If
k\geq 2
, then (
5.12
) with
k-1
in place of
k
implies (
5.12
) since
\mathcal{Z}
has increments in
\{(1,-1),(-1,0),(0,1)\}
.

By the KMSW procedure, this means that at time
N_{k}^{R}
we add a new non-missing edge (rather than a triangular face) to
M_{0,N_{k}^{R}-1}
. This edge is
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}}
.
By the definition of
N_{k}^{R}
, the edge
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}}
lies in
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
. Furthermore, the edge on the boundary of
M_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
immediately to the west of
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}}
is
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}-1}
, which (by the definition of
N_{k}^{R}
) is not in
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
.
Hence
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}-1}=(x,\lambda^{-}_{N_{k}^{R}})\notin\mathcal{E}(\widehat{M}_{0,\infty})
and so
x\notin\mathcal{V}(\widehat{M}_{0,\infty})
.

By the definition of
N_{k}^{L}
, the time
N_{k}^{L}
is at most the first time
n\geq N_{k}^{R}+1
at which
x
is a vertex of
\lambda_{n}
. From Assertion
(i)
of Lemma
3.6
(applied to the walk
(\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R}+\cdot)-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R}))|_{[0,\infty)}
and the corresponding map
M_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
), this time is

\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\geq N_{k}^{R}+1:\mathcal{L}(n)=\mathcal{L}(N_{k}^{R})-1}}\right\}.

(5.14)

Conversely, assume that
n\geq N_{k}^{R}+1
and
n
is strictly smaller than the time (
5.14
).
We make the following preliminary observation: Fix
m\geq 0
. Let
N>m
be the first time such that
\mathcal{L}(N)<\mathcal{L}(m)
. Thanks to Assertion
(i)
of Lemma
3.6
(applied to the walk
(\mathcal{Z}(m+\cdot)-\mathcal{Z}(m))|_{[0,N-1)}
and the corresponding map
M_{m,N-1}
), the map
M_{m,N-1}
does not have any edge on the lower-left boundary.
Hence, the vertex
\lambda^{-}_{m}
remains on the lower-left boundary of the map
M_{m^{\prime},\infty}
for all times
m^{\prime}
such that
m\leq m^{\prime}<N
.

Since we assume that
n
is strictly smaller than the time (
5.14
), we deduce from the latter observation that
\lambda^{-}_{N_{k}^{R}}
lies on the lower-left boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
, i.e. to the boundary to the west of
\lambda_{n}
(possibly
\lambda^{-}_{N_{k}^{R}}
is the initial vertex of
\lambda_{n}
).
Moreover, there is a directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
from
\lambda^{-}_{N_{k}^{R}}
to the initial vertex of
\lambda_{n}
, namely a segment of the boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
. Therefore,
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
because
\widehat{M}_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
is, by definition, the submap of
M_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
induced by the vertices of
M_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
which are reachable by a directed path in
M_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}
started from the initial vertex
\lambda^{-}_{N_{k}^{R}}
. Since
\widehat{M}_{N_{k}^{R},\infty}\subset\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
, we conclude that
n<N_{k}^{L}
. Hence
N_{k}^{L}
is equal to the time (
5.14
), as stated in (
5.12
), and we also proved Assertion
(i)
.

Now we consider
N_{k+1}^{R}
. Let
y
be the vertex on the boundary of
M_{N_{k}^{L},\infty}
lying immediately to the east of the terminal vertex
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
of
\lambda_{N_{k}^{L}}
. By the description of
N_{k}^{L}
proven just above, we have (
5.13
) since
\mathcal{Z}
has increments in
\{(1,-1),(-1,0),(0,1)\}
.
By the KMSW procedure, at time
N_{k}^{L}
, we add a triangle with two of its edges on the boundary of
M_{N_{k}^{L}-1,\infty}
, and
\lambda_{N_{k}^{L}}
is the other edge of this triangle. In particular,
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
is equal to
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}-1}
, which is in
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
. Since
\lambda_{N_{k}^{L}}\notin\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
, the vertex
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
must in fact lie on the left boundary
\beta_{0}
of
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
. Furthermore, we note the following fact for future reference: since
\beta_{0}
cannot cross the left or right boundaries of
M_{N_{k}^{L},\infty}
, the part of
\beta_{0}
contained in
M_{N_{k}^{L},\infty}
consists only of edges of
\beta_{0}
traversed after it hits
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
.

The edge
(\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}},y)
is directed, and so, since
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
, this edge also belongs to
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
.
By the definition of
N_{k+1}^{R}
, we thus have that
N_{k+1}^{R}
is at most the first time
n\geq N_{k}^{L}+1
for which
\lambda_{n}=(\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}},y)
. By Assertion
(ii)
of Lemma
3.6
(applied to the walk
(\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}+\cdot)-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}))|_{[0,\infty)}
and the corresponding map
M_{N_{k}^{L},\infty}
), this time is

\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\geq N_{k}^{L}+1:\mathcal{R}(n)=\mathcal{R}(N_{k}^{L})-1}}\right\}.

(5.15)

Conversely, if
n\geq N_{k}^{L}+1
and
n
is strictly smaller than the time (
5.15
),
then
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
lies on the lower-left boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
, i.e. to the boundary to the east of
\lambda_{n}
(possibly
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
is the terminal vertex of
\lambda_{n}
); this follows using the same idea we used below (
5.14
).
We also make the following observation for future reference: the segment of the boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
from
\lambda^{+}_{n}
to
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
is a directed path between these two vertices in
M_{0,\infty}
. Since the orientation on
M_{-\infty,\infty}
is acyclic, there is no directed path in
M_{-\infty,\infty}
from
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
to
\lambda^{+}_{n}
. Since
(\lambda^{-}_{n},\lambda^{+}_{n})
is directed, there is also no directed path in
M_{-\infty,\infty}
from
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
to
\lambda^{-}_{n}
.

We now show that
N_{k+1}^{R}>n
. Since
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}-1}\notin\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
,
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
, and
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}-1}
and
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}}
share a vertex, at least one of the vertices of
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}}
must lie on
\beta_{0}
(we will see a posteriori that
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}}^{-}
has to be the shared vertex, but for the moment it may be
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}}^{-}
or
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}}^{+}
). As we noted at the end of the paragraph after (
5.13
),
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
is the first vertex of
M_{N_{k}^{L},\infty}
hit by
\beta_{0}
. So, one of the vertices of
\lambda_{N_{k+1}^{R}}
is hit by
\beta_{0}
after
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
. Since
\beta_{0}
is a directed path, this gives a directed path from
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k}^{L}}
to either
\lambda^{-}_{N_{k+1}^{R}}
or
\lambda^{+}_{N_{k+1}^{R}}
, which combined with the result in the previous paragraph shows that
N_{k+1}^{R}>n
. Hence
N_{k+1}^{R}
is equal to the time (
5.15
), as stated in (
5.12
), and we also proved Assertion
(ii)
.
∎

We will now describe the KMSW encoding walks for the maps
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
in terms of
\mathcal{Z}
.
For
m\in\mathbbm{N}_{0}
, let
S_{m},S_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{N}_{0}
be the smallest integers such that

m=\#\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\leq S_{m}:\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}}}\right\}=\#\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\leq S_{m}^{\prime}:\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}}}\right\}.

(5.16)

Define
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}=(\widehat{\mathcal{L}},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}):\mathbbm{N}_{0}\to\mathbbm{Z}
by
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(0)=(0,0)
and

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(m)-\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(m-1):=\mathcal{Z}(S_{m})-\mathcal{Z}(S_{m}-1),\quad\forall m\in\mathbbm{N}.

(5.17)

In other words,
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(m)
is obtained by summing the first
m
increments of
\mathcal{Z}
corresponding to times
n
such that
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
.

Similarly, with
N_{k}^{L}
as in Lemma
5.12
, we define
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}=(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}):\mathbbm{N}_{0}\to\mathbbm{Z}
so that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(0)=(0,0)
and

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(m)-\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(m-1):=\begin{cases}\mathcal{Z}(S^{\prime}_{m})-\mathcal{Z}(S^{\prime}_{m}-1),\quad S^{\prime}_{m}\notin\{N_{k}^{L}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{N}}\\
(0,1),\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\,\,S^{\prime}_{m}\in\{N_{k}^{L}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{N}},\end{cases}\quad\forall m\in\mathbbm{N}.

(5.18)

In other words,
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(m)
is obtained by summing the first
m
increments of
\mathcal{Z}
corresponding to times
n
such that
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
, except that for each of the times
N_{k}^{L}
, we replace the corresponding increment

\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}-1)=(-1,0)
by
(0,1)
. The reason we do this is explained in the proof of the next lemma.

Lemma 5.13
.

The maps
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
are obtained by applying the KMSW procedure (Definition
3.3
) to the walks
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
, respectively.

Proof.

The fact that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
(resp.
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
) is the correct KMSW walk for
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
(resp.
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
) is obvious when
S_{m}\notin\{N_{k}^{R}\}_{k\geq 2}
(resp.
S^{\prime}_{m}\notin\{N_{k}^{L}\}_{k\geq 1}
), i.e. when the KMSW exploration of
M_{0,\infty}
is not crossing
\beta_{0}
.
We now justify why the definitions in (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) are the correct definitions also when
S_{m}\in\{N_{k}^{R}\}_{k\geq 2}
and
S^{\prime}_{m}\in\{N_{k}^{L}\}_{k\geq 1}
. We first look at the case
S^{\prime}_{m}\in\{N_{k}^{L}\}_{k\geq 1}
.

By Assertion
(ii)
of Lemma
5.12
, at each of the times
N_{k}^{L}
for
k\geq 1
, the KMSW procedure for
M_{0,\infty}
creates a triangular face
T
contained in
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
whose boundary consists of the edge
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}-1}
, the (new active) edge
\lambda_{N_{k}^{L}}
, and the (previously active) edge
e=\lambda_{N_{k}^{L}-1}
along the path
\beta_{0}
; see the triangle to the west of
\beta_{0}
in the light-blue region in Figure
14
. Now, since the edges along
\beta_{0}
are missing edges in
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
, the KMSW procedure for
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
never activates the missing edge
e
. On the other hand, the KMSW procedure for
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
adds the triangular face
T
by adding it directly to the edge
\lambda_{N^{R}_{k}-1}
and creating the new (non-missing) edge
\lambda_{N^{L}_{k}}
and the new (missing) edge
e
. This corresponds to a
(0,1)
step for the KMSW encoding walk of
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
(instead of a
(-1,0)
step for the KMSW encoding walk of
M_{0,\infty}
).

We finally explain why, for the walk
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
in (
5.17
), we do not have to make a modification similar to the one we had to make for
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
in (
5.18
).
By Assertion
(i)
of Lemma
5.12
, at each of the times
N_{k}^{R}
for
k\geq 2
, the KMSW procedure for
M_{0,\infty}
adds at time
N_{k}^{R}
a new single non-missing edge (rather than a triangular face) to
M_{0,N_{k}^{R}-1}
. This edge is
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}}
; see the pink edge on the lower boundary of the light-blue region in Figure
14
. Now, noting that the KMSW procedure for
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
must add the edge
\lambda_{N_{k}^{R}}
using the same procedure, i.e. adding a new single non-missing edge (rather than a triangular face), we get that the step (1,-1) taken by the
\mathcal{Z}
walk is the correct step also for the
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
walk.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The walks
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
can equivalently be described in terms of the times
N_{k}^{R},N_{k}^{L}
for
k\geq 1
defined in Lemma
5.12
. Indeed, by definition of
N_{k}^{R}
and
N_{k}^{L}
, the collections of times
n
such that
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\lambda_{n}\in\widehat{M}^{\prime}_{0,\infty}
are respectively

\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}[N_{k}^{R},N_{k}^{L}-1]\cap\mathbbm{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}[N_{k}^{L},N_{k+1}^{R}-1]\cap\mathbbm{Z}.

(5.19)

By (
5.17
) and (
5.19
),
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
can be obtained by concatenating the random walk paths

\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,N_{1}^{L}-1]}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{R}-1)-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R}-1)}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k}^{L}-N_{k}^{R}]},\quad\text{for }k\geq 2.

(5.20)

That is, to get
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(m)
we sum the first
m
increments of these paths in order. Similarly, by (
5.18
) and (
5.19
),
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
can be obtained by concatenating the random walk paths

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{L}-1)-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}-1)+(1,1)\,\mathbbm{1}_{[1,\infty)}(\cdot)}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k+1}^{R}-N_{k}^{L}]}\quad\text{for }k\geq 1.

(5.21)

Here, adding
(1,1)\,\mathbbm{1}_{[1,\infty)}(\cdot)
is equivalent to replacing the first walk step
\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ref-for-lat-disc}}}{{=}}(-1,0)
of each walk path by
(0,1)
; which accounts for the special rule at the times
N_{k}^{L}
in (
5.18
).

The following lemma is the key input in the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The walks
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
of (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) are independent. The walk
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
has the same law as the walk obtained by conditioning
\mathcal{Z}
so that its first coordinate stays non-negative (defined as in (
4.1
)). As for the walk
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
, we have
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(0)=(0,0)
,
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(1)=(1,0)
, and
(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(\cdot+1)-\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(1))|_{[0,\infty)}
has the same law as the walk obtained by conditioning
\mathcal{Z}
so that its second coordinate stays non-negative (defined as in (
4.1
) with
\mathcal{R}
in place of
\mathcal{L}
).

For the proof of Lemma
5.14
, we need the following random walk analog of Pitman’s theorem for Brownian motion.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a random walk with i.i.d. increments which are uniform on
\{-1,0,1\}
. For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, define

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(n):=\mathcal{L}(n)-2\min_{j\in[0,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}\mathcal{L}(j).

(5.22)

In words, to obtain
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
, every time
\mathcal{L}
attains a running minimum (at which time it has a
-1
step), we flip the
-1
step to a
+1
step.
Then
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
is a random walk started from 0 and conditioned to stay non-negative in the sense of
[
BD94
]
(see (
4.1
)).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{n}
be the set of
n
-step paths
\mathfrak{L}:[0,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}
such that
\mathfrak{L}(0)=0
and
\mathfrak{L}(j)-\mathfrak{L}(j-1)\in\{-1,0,1\}
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Let
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{n}
be the set of
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{W}_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{L}(j)\geq 0
for each
j\in\{0,\dots,n\}
. We define a function
\Phi_{n}:\mathcal{W}_{n}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{n}
by

\Phi_{n}\mathfrak{L}(j):=\mathfrak{L}(j)-2\min_{i\in[0,j]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}\mathfrak{L}(i).

In words, to obtain
\Phi_{n}\mathfrak{L}
, every time
\mathfrak{L}
attains a running minimum (at which time it has a
-1
step), we flip the
-1
step to a
+1
step.

For
\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{n}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(n)\}
, we also define
\Psi_{n,k}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathcal{W}_{n}
by

\Psi_{n,k}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(j):=\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(j)-2\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{k,\min_{i\in[j,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(i)}}\right\}.

In words, to obtain
\Psi_{n,k}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}
, for
r\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, we look at the last time that
\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}
hits
r
before time
n
, at which time it has a
+1
step. We flip each of these
+1
steps to a
-1
step.

From the step-flipping descriptions of
\Phi_{n}
and
\Psi_{n,k}
, for any
\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{n}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(k)\}
, we have
\Phi_{n}\Psi_{n,k}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}=\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}
. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{W}_{n}
and
k=\min_{j\in[0,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}\mathcal{L}(j)
, then
\Psi_{n,k}\Phi_{n}\mathfrak{L}=\mathfrak{L}
. These two facts imply that if
\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{n}
, then

\Phi_{n}^{-1}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Psi_{n,k}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}:k\in\{0,\dots,\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(n)\}}}\right\}.

Consequently,
\#\Phi_{n}^{-1}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}=\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(n)+1
.

For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, the walk
\mathcal{L}|_{[0,n]}
is a uniform sample from
\mathcal{W}_{n}
. We have
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}|_{[0,n]}=\Phi_{n}(\mathcal{L}|_{[0,n]})
, so the previous paragraph implies that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}|_{[0,n]}
is sampled from the uniform measure on
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{n}
weighted by
\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(n)+1
. By the definition (
4.1
), this means that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
is the walk obtained by conditioning
\mathcal{L}
to stay non-negative.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.14
.

By Lemma
5.12
and the strong Markov property, the sequences of random walk paths

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k+1}^{R}-N_{k}^{L}]}}}\right\}_{k\geq 1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{R})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R})}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k}^{L}-N_{k}^{R}]}}}\right\}_{k\geq 1}

(5.23)

are independent. By definition of
N_{k}^{L}
and
N_{k}^{R}
in Lemma
5.12
, the first (resp. second) sequence in (
5.23
) has the law of a collection of i.i.d. samples from the law of
\mathcal{Z}
stopped at the first time that
\mathcal{R}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}
) hits
-1
.
Consequently, concatenating the elements of the first (resp. second) sequence in (
5.23
) in order gives a walk
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}=(\mathring{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime},\mathring{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})
(resp.
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}=(\mathring{\mathcal{L}},\mathring{\mathcal{R}})
) with the same law as
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\infty)}
. Furthermore, the walks
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
are independent.

The
k
th path
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k+1}^{R}-N_{k}^{L}]}
from the first sequence in (
5.23
) coincides with the segment of
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
between the first time that
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}
hits
-(k-1)
and the first time that
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}
hits
-k
.
Similarly, the
k
th path
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{R})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R})}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k}^{L}-N_{k}^{R}]}
from the second sequence in (
5.23
) coincides with the segment of
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}
between the first time that
\mathring{\mathcal{L}}
hits
-(k-1)
and the first time that
\mathring{\mathcal{L}}
hits
-k
.

From (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) in Lemma
5.12
, we have that the last step of the two paths

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k+1}^{R}-N_{k}^{L}]}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+N_{k}^{R})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{R})}}\right)|_{[0,N_{k}^{L}-N_{k}^{R}]}

are respectively

\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{R})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{R}-1)=(1,-1)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}-1)=(-1,0).

Hence, recalling (
5.21
),
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
in the following manner. We set
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
to be the walk starting at
(0,0)
, taking a first
(1,0)
step (this is because the first walk path in (
5.21
) has an additional starting
(1,0)
step compared to the first walk path in the first sequence in (
5.23
)), and then exactly following the steps of the following modified version of
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
. For each of the times at which
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
attains a running minimum, replace the corresponding step
\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{R})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{R}-1)=(1,-1)
, which is the last step of the
k
th path in the first sequence in (
5.23
), by the step
\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{L}-1)+(1,1)=(0,1)
, which is the first step of the
k+1
th excursion in (
5.21
).

Similarly, recalling (
5.20
), we find that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
can be obtained from
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}
in the following manner. For each of the times at which
\mathring{\mathcal{L}}
attains a running minimum, replace the corresponding step
\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k}^{L}-1)=(-1,0)
, which is the last step of the
k
th excursion in the second sequence in (
5.23
), by the step
\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{R})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{k+1}^{R}-1)=(1,-1)
, which is the first step of the
k+1
th excursion in (
5.20
). Note that in this case we do not need to do any special modification for the first step of the walk since the first step of the first walk path in (
5.20
) is
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)
and the first step of the first walk path in the second sequence in (
5.23
) is
\mathcal{Z}(N_{1}^{R}+1)-\mathcal{Z}(N_{1}^{R})=\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)
since
N_{1}^{R}=0
by Lemma
5.12
.

Since
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
are independent and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
are functions of
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\mathring{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
, respectively,
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
are independent.

The marginal law of
\mathring{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
is that of a random walk with i.i.d. increments each sampled uniformly from
\{-1,0,1\}
. Therefore, the previous paragraph together with Lemma
5.15
implies that
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
has the same law as
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
conditioned to stay non-negative and taking a first
0
step (this is because of the first
(1,0)
step of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
). Since the steps of
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
determine the steps of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
, this implies that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
has the desired law.
Similarly, we obtain that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
has the desired law.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Thanks to Lemma
5.13
, the maps
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
can be built using the KMSW procedure on the walks
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
of Lemma
5.14
, respectively. Hence, Lemma
5.14
implies that
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
and
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}^{\prime}
are independent. Furthermore, by Definition
4.1
and the description of the law of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
from Lemma
5.14
, we get that
\widehat{M}_{0,\infty}
has the law of the UIQBOT.
∎

5.6
Proof of Proposition
5.9
: Symmetry for the UIBHBOT

Recall the set of boundary-channeled bipolar-oriented triangulations
\mathcal{M}_{l,r}^{\operatorname{b}}
with
l
left boundary edges and
r
right boundary edges from Definition
3.17
.
For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
\mathcal{M}_{l,r}^{\operatorname{b}}(n)
be the set of maps in
\mathcal{M}_{l,r}^{\operatorname{b}}
having
n
non-boundary edges.
To prove Proposition
5.9
, we will first show that the UIBHBOT arises as the local limit of uniformly sampled elements of
\mathcal{M}_{l,1}^{\operatorname{b}}(n)
. Proposition
5.9
will then be an easy consequence of the left/right symmetry property of
\mathcal{M}_{l,r}^{\operatorname{b}}
(Lemma
3.18
).

The following proposition justifies the terminology in Definition
5.8
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
\{l_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
be a sequence of positive integers. Assume there exists
A>1
such that
l_{n}\in[A^{-1}n^{1/2},An^{1/2}]
for each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
.
For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
be the map obtained by starting with a uniform sample from
\mathcal{M}_{l_{n}+1,1}^{\operatorname{b}}(n-1)
(as defined just above), then declaring all of its left boundary edges, except for the left boundary edge whose terminal vertex is the sink vertex, to be missing.
Conditional on
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
, let
e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
be a uniform sample from the
l_{n}
missing edges on the (lower-left) boundary of
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
.
Then
(M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})
converges in law to the UIBHBOT of Definition
5.8
with respect to the (directed) Benjamini-Schramm topology (Definition
5.8
) .

We will prove Proposition
5.16
via a standard argument based on Cramér’s large deviation theorem
[
DZ10
, Theorem 2.1.24]
; see, e.g.,
[
DS11
, Section 4.2]
for a similar argument.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}=(\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})
be the walk which encodes
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
via the KMSW bijection as in Lemma
3.19
.
By Lemma
3.19
, the walk
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
is a uniform sample from the set of walks with increments in the set
\{(1,-1),(-1,0),(0,1)\}
which start at
(l_{n},0)
, stay in
[0,\infty)^{2}
until time
n
, and end up at
(0,0)
at time
n
. Let
K_{n}\in[1,l_{n}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
be chosen so that
e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
is the
K_{n}
th missing edge in order on the lower-left boundary of
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
(starting from the missing edge next to the unique lower-right boundary edge). Since
e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
is chosen uniformly from the missing boundary edges of
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
, the integer
K_{n}
is a uniform sample from
[1,l_{n}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
, independent from
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
. Let
T_{n}
be the stopping time

T_{n}:=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{j\in[1,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}:\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}(j)-\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}(0)=-K_{n}}}\right\}.

(5.24)

By Assertion
(i)
of Lemma
3.6
,
T_{n}
is the same as the time at which the KMSW exploration associated with
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
explores the triangle of
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
with
e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
on its boundary.

The main technical step in the proof of Proposition
5.16
is to show that the probability of the event that we condition on to get the law of
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
decays slower than exponentially in
l_{n}
. We will in fact prove a power-law lower bound.

Lemma 5.17
.

Recall from Section
4
that
\mathbbm{P}_{(l,0)}
denotes the law of a walk
\mathcal{Z}
with i.i.d. increments sampled uniformly from
\{(1,-1),(-1,0),(0,1)\}
, started from
(l,0)
.
For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let

E_{n}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{Z}[0,n]\subset[0,\infty)^{2},\>\mathcal{Z}(n)=(0,0)}}\right\}.

(5.25)

For each
A>1
, there exist
c>0
such that

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}[E_{n}]\geq cn^{-3},\quad\forall n\in\mathbbm{N},\quad\forall l_{n}\in[A^{-1}n^{1/2},An^{1/2}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}.

(5.26)

Proof.

This is a fairly straightforward consequence of the results of
[
DW15a
,
DW15b
]
, but these results do not exactly apply in our setting so we will explain how to extract (
5.26
) from the results of
[
DW15a
,
DW15b
]
. Whenever we apply results from
[
DW15a
,
DW15b
]
, we need to apply an affine transformation which maps
\mathcal{Z}
to an uncorrelated random walk, see
[
DW15a
, Example 2]
. In our setting, the correlation of the coordinates of
\mathcal{Z}
is
-1/2
, so the parameter
p
from
[
DW15a
,
DW15b
]
is
3
.

It suffices to prove the lemma only for
n\geq 100
.
Let
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\in\mathbbm{N}
be chosen so that

n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n,\quad n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geq n/4,\quad n_{2}\in 3\mathbbm{Z}.

(5.27)

The reason for requiring that
n_{2}\in 3\mathbbm{Z}
is that
\mathcal{Z}
is periodic of period 3.

We will lower-bound the probabilities of three events, depending on the restrictions of
\mathcal{Z}
to intervals of length
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}
, whose intersection is contained in
E_{n}
.
To this end, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{1}(j)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{Z}(j),\quad j\in[0,n_{1}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{2}(j)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{Z}(j+n_{1})-\mathcal{Z}(n_{1}),\quad j\in[0,n_{2}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{3}(j)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{Z}(n-j)-\mathcal{Z}(n),\quad j\in[0,n_{3}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}

Then
\mathcal{Z}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{2}
, and
\mathcal{Z}^{3}
are independent, and the laws of
\mathcal{Z}^{2}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{3}
do not depend on the starting point for
\mathcal{Z}
.

Define the events

\displaystyle E_{n}^{1}

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{Z}^{1}(j)\in[0,\infty)^{2},\>\forall j\in[0,n_{1}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}}\right\}\cap\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{Z}^{1}(n_{1})\in[n^{1/2},2n^{1/2}]^{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle E_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{Z}^{2}(j)+\mathcal{Z}^{1}(n_{1})\in[0,\infty)^{2},\>\forall j\in[0,n_{2}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}}\right\}\cap\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{Z}^{2}(n_{2})=\mathcal{Z}^{3}(n_{3})-\mathcal{Z}^{1}(n_{1})}}\right\}

\displaystyle E_{n}^{3}

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{Z}^{3}(j)\in[0,\infty)^{2},\>\forall j\in[0,n_{3}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}}\right\}\cap\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{Z}^{3}(n_{3})\in[3n^{1/2},4n^{1/2}]^{2}}}\right\}.

Then
E_{n}^{1}\cap E_{n}^{2}\cap E_{n}^{3}\subset E_{n}
.
We also note that
E_{n}^{1}
and
E_{n}^{3}
are determined by
\mathcal{Z}^{1}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{3}
, respectively, so these events are independent.

We first consider the event
E_{n}^{1}
. By a standard estimate for one-dimensional random walk, there exists
c_{1}>0
such that for all
l,n\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\mathbbm{P}_{(0,0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{n}^{1}}}\right]\geq c_{1}n^{-1/2},\quad\text{where}\quad F_{n}^{1}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{R}(j)\geq 0,\>\forall j\in[0,n_{1}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}}\right\}.

(5.28)

By
[
DW15b
, Theorem 1]
, applied with the cone equal to the upper half-plane, under
\mathbbm{P}_{(0,0)}[\cdot\,|\,F_{n}^{1}]
, the re-scaled walk
n_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{Z}(\lfloor n_{1}\cdot\rfloor)|_{[0,1]}
converges as
n_{1}\to\infty
to a correlated two-dimensional Brownian motion (with correlation
-1/2
) started at
(0,0)
and conditioned to stay in the upper half-plane for one unit of time.
For any
l_{n}\in[A^{-1}n^{1/2},An^{1/2}]
and each small enough
\varepsilon>0
(depending on
A
), such a conditioned Brownian motion has a positive chance (uniformly over the choice of
l_{n}
) to stay in
[-n_{1}^{-1/2}l_{n}+\varepsilon,\infty)\times[0,\infty)
until time 1 and end up in
(-n_{1}^{-1/2}l_{n},0)+[2(1+\varepsilon),\sqrt{2}(2-\varepsilon)]^{2}
at time 1.
By shifting by
(l_{n},0)
, noting that
n^{1/2}/2\leq n_{1}^{1/2}\leq{n^{1/2}/\sqrt{2}}
by (
5.27
), and applying the preceding sentence together with (
5.28
), we find that there exists
c_{2}>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbbm{N}
and
l_{n}\in[A^{-1}n^{1/2},An^{1/2}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
,

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[E_{n}^{1}}}\right]\geq c_{2}n^{-1/2}.

(5.29)

Next we consider
E_{n}^{3}
.
By
[
DW15a
, Theorem 1]
, applied to the time reversal of
\mathcal{Z}
, there exists
c_{3}>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{Z}^{3}(j)\in[0,\infty)^{2},\>\forall j\in[0,n_{3}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}}\right]\geq c_{3}n^{-3/2}.

(5.30)

By
[
DW15b
, Theorem 1]
, applied as in the argument just above (
5.29
), this implies that we can find
c_{4}>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbbm{N}
and
l_{n}\in[A^{-1}n^{1/2},An^{1/2}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
,

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[E_{n}^{3}}}\right]\geq c_{4}n^{-3/2}.

(5.31)

To deal with
E_{n}^{2}
, we first observe that on
E_{n}^{1}\cap E_{n}^{3}
,

\mathcal{Z}^{3}(n_{3})-\mathcal{Z}^{1}(n_{1})\in[n^{1/2},3n^{1/2}]^{2}.

Recall that
\mathcal{Z}^{2}
is independent from
(\mathcal{Z}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{2})
.
The walk
\mathcal{Z}
is periodic of period 3, but the walk
\mathcal{Z}^{3}(3\cdot)
is strongly aperiodic since
\mathbbm{P}_{(0,0)}[\mathcal{Z}(3)=0]>0

[
Spi76
, Section 5, P1]
.
Since
n_{2}
was chosen to be a multiple of 3, we can apply a standard local limit result for unconditioned random walk (see, e.g.,
[
Spi76
, Section 7, P9]
) to get that there exists
c_{5}>0
such that on
E_{n}^{1}\cap E_{n}^{3}
,

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{Z}^{2}(n_{2})=\mathcal{Z}^{3}(n_{3})-\mathcal{Z}^{1}(n_{1})\,\middle|\ \mathcal{Z}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{2}}}\right]\geq c_{5}n^{-1}.

(5.32)

We now use the convergence of random walk conditioned on its endpoints to two-dimensional Brownian bridge (see, e.g.,
[
Lig70
, Theorem 3]
), together with the fact that for any
S>1
and
z,w\in[1/S,S]^{2}
, a (correlated) Brownian bridge from
z
to
w
has a uniformly positive chance to stay in
[0,\infty)^{2}
. We infer from (
5.32
) that there exists
c_{6}>0
such that on
E_{n}^{1}\cap E_{n}^{3}
,

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[E_{n}^{2}\,\middle|\ \mathcal{Z}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{2}}}\right]\geq c_{6}n^{-1}.

(5.33)

Combining (
5.29
), (
5.31
), and (
5.33
) and recalling that
E_{n}^{1}
and
E_{n}^{3}
are independent concludes the proof.
∎

Recall the discussion above (
5.24
).

Lemma 5.18
.

For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
E_{n}
be as in (
5.25
).
Let
\{l_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
be a sequence of positive integers. Assume there exists
A>1
such that
l_{n}\in[A^{-1}n^{1/2},An^{1/2}]
for each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}=(\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})
be a walk from
(l_{n},0)
to
(0,0)
in
[0,\infty)^{2}
sampled from the
\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}
-conditional law of
\mathcal{Z}
given
E_{n}
.
Also let
K_{n}
be sampled uniformly from
[1,l_{n}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
, independently from
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
and let
T_{n}
be as in (
5.24
).
For each fixed
N\in\mathbbm{N}
, the law of

\Big(\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}(\cdot+T_{n})-\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}(T_{n})\Big)|_{[-N,N]}

(5.34)

converges in the total variation sense as
n\to\infty
to the law of
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}|_{[-N,N]}
, where
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
is the encoding walk for the UIBHBOT, as defined at the beginning of Section
5.4
.

Proof.

Fix
m\in\mathbbm{N}
. We will send
n\to\infty
then send
m\to\infty
. We first study the unconditional law of
\mathcal{Z}
sampled from
\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}
.
Let
\sigma_{0}=0
and for
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, let

\sigma_{k}:=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{j\in\mathbbm{N}:\mathcal{L}(j)-\mathcal{L}(0)=-k}}\right\}.

(5.35)

Note that
T_{n}
in (
5.24
) is equal to
\sigma_{K_{n}}
(with
\mathcal{L}
replaced by
\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
).
Furthermore, for
s\in\mathbbm{N}
, the stopped walk paths

Z_{s}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+\sigma_{(s-1)m})-\mathcal{Z}(\sigma_{(s-1)m})}}\right)|_{[0,(\sigma_{sm}-\sigma_{(s-1)m})\wedge m^{100}]}

(5.36)

are i.i.d. paths. The reason why we stop after
m^{100}
steps is to ensure that
Z_{s}
takes values in a finite set (namely, the set of paths in
\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
started from
(0,0)
with increments in
\{(1,-1),(0,1),(-1,0)\}
which go for at most
m^{100}
steps). We also note that the definition of the stopping times
\sigma_{k}
in (
5.35
) and the increments in (
5.36
) does not depend on the starting point for
\mathcal{Z}
.

By Cramér’s larger deviation theorem
[
DZ10
, Theorem 2.1.24]
, the empirical distribution for
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{\lfloor l_{n}/m\rfloor}
is exponentially concentrated around the actual distribution of
Z_{1}
as
n\to\infty
. That is, for any possible realization
\mathfrak{z}
of
Z_{1}
, let
p_{l_{n}}(\mathfrak{z})
be the fraction of values of
s\in[1,l_{n}/m]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
for which
Z_{s}=\mathfrak{z}
. Then for each
\delta>0
, there exists
c=c(m,\delta)>0
such that
15
15

15

The law of
Z_{s}
in (
5.36
) does not depend on
s
, so (
5.37
) is equivalent to the analogous statement with
\mathbbm{P}_{(r,0)}
in place of
\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}
for any
r\in\mathbbm{Z}
.

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sup_{\mathfrak{z}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|p_{l_{n}}(\mathfrak{z})-\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}[Z_{1}=\mathfrak{z}]}}\right|>\delta}}\right]=O(e^{-c\,l_{n}}),\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$},

(5.37)

where the supremum is over all possible realizations
\mathfrak{z}
of
Z_{1}
.

By Lemma
5.17
,
\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}[E_{n}]
decays like a negative power of
n
. In particular, since
l_{n}
is comparable to
n^{1/2}
, this probability decays slower than exponentially in
l_{n}
. Hence (
5.38
) implies that
