\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sup_{\mathfrak{z}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|p_{l_{n}}(\mathfrak{z})-\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}[Z_{1}=\mathfrak{z}]}}\right|>\delta\,\middle|\,E_{n}}}\right]=O(e^{-c^{\prime}l_{n}}),\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$},

(5.38)

for a slightly smaller constant
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}(\delta,m)>0
.

Let
K_{n}
be as in the lemma statement and let
S_{n}\in[1,l_{n}/m]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
be chosen so that
K_{n}\in[(S_{n}-1)m,S_{n}m]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
(we take
S_{n}
to be a graveyard point in the probability space if
K_{n}\in[m\lfloor l_{n}/m\rfloor+1,l_{n}]
).
By (
5.38
), the total variation distance between the conditional law of
Z_{S_{n}}
given
E_{n}
and the unconditional law of
Z_{1}
goes to zero as
n\to\infty
(it would in fact decay exponentially, it not for the possibility that
K_{n}\in[m\lfloor l_{n}/m\rfloor+1,l_{n}]
).

It remains to transfer from a statement about the law of
Z_{S_{n}}
to a statement about the law of (
5.34
).
To this end, let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
N\in\mathbbm{N}
.
Since
\sigma_{sm}
is the hitting time of
-sm
for the lazy random walk
\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}(0)
, if we choose
m
large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
) then for every
s\in[1,l_{n}/m]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
, the unconditional
\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}
-probability that
\sigma_{sm}-\sigma_{(s-1)m}
is larger than
m^{100}
is at most
\varepsilon/2
. By the previous paragraph, if
m
is chosen to be large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
) and
n
is large enough (depending on
m
and
\varepsilon
), then

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma_{S_{n}m}-\sigma_{(S_{n}-1)m}\leq m^{100}\,\middle|\,E_{n}}}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(5.39)

Furthermore, if
m
is large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
and
N
) and
n
is large enough (depending on
N
,
m
, and
\varepsilon
), then the conditional probability given
E_{n}
that
[K_{n}-N,K_{n}+N]\subset[(S_{n}-1)m,S_{n}m]
is at least
1-\varepsilon
.
By this and (
5.39
), we can choose
m=m(\varepsilon,N)\in\mathbbm{N}
large enough so that when
n
is sufficiently large (recall that
T_{n}
in (
5.24
) is equal to
\sigma_{K_{n}}
),

\mathbbm{P}_{(l_{n},0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[[T_{n}-N,T_{n}+N]\subset\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma_{(S_{n}-1)m},\sigma_{(S_{n}-1)m}+(\sigma_{S_{n}m}-\sigma_{(S_{n}-1)m})\wedge m^{100}}}\right]\,\middle|\,E_{n}}}\right]\geq 1-2\varepsilon.

In other words, under the conditional law given
E_{n}
, it holds with probability at least
1-2\varepsilon
that the walk path (
5.34
) (defined with
\mathcal{Z}
in place of
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
) is determined by
Z_{S_{n}}
and
K_{n}-(S_{n}-1)m
. By combining this with the previous paragraph and recalling that
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
is sampled from the conditional law of
\mathcal{Z}
given
E_{n}
, we get the following. When
n
and
m
are sufficiently large, the total variation distance between the laws of the following two processes is at most
2\varepsilon
: the process (
5.34
) and the law of
(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+\sigma_{M})-\mathcal{Z}(\sigma_{M}))|_{[-N,N]}
, where
M
is sampled uniformly from
[0,m]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
(recall the definition of
Z_{1}
from (
5.36
). By the construction of
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
at the beginning of Section
5.4
, this concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.16
.

This is an easy consequence of Lemma
5.18
, but we spell out the details for completeness.
Recall the discussion above (
5.24
).
Let
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
be the UIBHBOT and let
\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}
be its KMSW encoding walk, as in the discussion just above Definition
5.8
.
For
a,b\in\mathbbm{Z}
with
a<b
, let
M_{a,b}^{\operatorname{b}}
be the submap of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
obtained by applying the KMSW procedure to
(\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(\cdot+a)-\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{b}}(a))|_{[a,b]}
.
For
r\in\mathbbm{N}
, define the
graph distance ball

\mathcal{B}_{r}(M^{\operatorname{b}})
to be the submap of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
consisting of the vertices of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
which lie at graph distance at most
r
from the initial vertex of
e^{\operatorname{b}}
, along with the edges of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
whose vertices both satisfy this condition, and the faces of
M^{\operatorname{b}}
whose boundary vertices all satisfy this condition.
For any
\varepsilon>0
and
r\in\mathbbm{N}
, we can choose
N=N(r,\varepsilon)\in\mathbbm{N}
sufficiently large so that

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{B}_{r}(M^{\operatorname{b}})\subset M_{-N,N}^{\operatorname{b}}}}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon.

By the total variation convergence in Lemma
5.18
, this implies that for large enough
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, it holds with probability at least
1-2\varepsilon
that the graph distance ball
\mathcal{B}_{r}(M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})
of radius
r
in
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
centered at the initial vertex of
e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
is completely
explored by the KMSW procedure for
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
during the time interval
[T_{n}-N,T_{n}+N]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
(the latter is determined by the walk
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}|_{[T_{n}-N,T_{n}+N]}
). Note that Lemma
5.18
guarantees that we can choose the same
N
for all values of
n
.
By applying Lemma
5.18
again, this implies the desired Benjamini-Schramm convergence.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.9
.

Assume that we are in the setting of Proposition
5.16
.
Let
(\widetilde{M}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})
be the edge-rooted directed map obtained from
(M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})
by applying an orientation-reversing homeomorphism from
\mathbbm{C}
to
\mathbbm{C}
.
By the definition of
M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
(see Proposition
5.16
), the map
\widetilde{M}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
can be obtained by starting with a uniform sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1,l_{n}+1}^{\operatorname{b}}(n)
(as defined just above Proposition
5.16
), then declaring all of the right boundary edges to be missing except for the one whose terminal vertex is the sink vertex.
Furthermore,
\widetilde{e}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
is a uniformly sampled missing boundary edge of
\widetilde{M}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}
.
By Lemma
3.18
(applied with
(l,r)=(1,l_{n}+1)
and
k=l_{n}
),

(M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})\overset{d}{=}(\widetilde{M}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}}).

(5.40)

By Proposition
5.16
,
(M_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},e_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})\to(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})
in law with respect to the Benjamini-Schramm topology. By applying an orientation-reversing homeomorphism, we get that also
(\widetilde{M}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}_{n}^{\operatorname{b}})\to(\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}^{\operatorname{b}})
in law with respect to the Benjamini-Schramm topology, where
(\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}^{\operatorname{b}})
is as in the proposition statement.
By (
5.40
), this shows that
(M^{\operatorname{b}},e^{\operatorname{b}})\overset{d}{=}(\widetilde{M}^{\operatorname{b}},\widetilde{e}^{\operatorname{b}})
, as required.
∎

6
Recursive equations, tail asymptotics, and scaling exponents

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.8
about the tail asymptotics of the increments of the Busemann function
\mathcal{X}
when
{\operatorname{XDP}}\in\{\operatorname{LDP},\operatorname{SDP}\}
. We will prove Items
(va)
and
(vb)
of Theorem
1.8
separately in Sections
6.1
and
6.2
, respectively.

Recall from Lemma
3.8
that for
n\in\mathbbm{Z}
, the infinite rooted bipolar-oriented triangulation with missing edges
(M_{n,\infty},\lambda_{n})
is the submap of the UIBOT obtained by applying the KMSW procedure to
\mathcal{Z}|_{[n,\infty)}
, where
\mathcal{Z}:\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
is a bi-infinite random walk whose increments are i.i.d. uniform samples from
\{(1,-1),(-1,0),(0,1)\}
, normalized so that
\mathcal{Z}(0)=(0,0)
.

By the stationarity of
\mathcal{Z}
, we have that

M_{1,\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{0,\infty}.

(6.1)

Therefore, the Busemann function
\mathcal{X}^{1}
associated with
M_{1,\infty}
has the same law as
\mathcal{X}
. Also, recall the notation

f(x):=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)=x}}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad g(y):=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)=y}}\right].

(6.2)

Since the Busemann function
\mathcal{X}
introduced in Theorem
1.4
is normalized so that
\mathcal{X}(0)=0
, we equivalently have that
f(x)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\mathcal{X}(-1)=x}}\right]
and
g(y)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)=y}}\right]
.
Our main goal will be to determine the tail behavior of
f
and
g
for
{\operatorname{XDP}}\in\{\operatorname{LDP},\operatorname{SDP}\}
.

Using the fact that
\mathcal{X}^{1}\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}
and the properties in Theorem
1.7
proved in the previous section, we will obtain a recursive equation for
f
and
g
(see Proposition
6.5
for the
\operatorname{LDP}
case and Proposition
6.11
for the
\operatorname{SDP}
case), which will later be converted into a functional equation for the characteristic functions
F
and
G
of
f
and
g
, respectively.
We then reach our goal by first studying the asymptotics of the characteristic functions
F
and
G
(see Proposition
6.2
for the
\operatorname{LDP}
case and Proposition
6.8
for the
\operatorname{SDP}
case) and then transferring the estimates to
f
and
g
using the following Tauberian type result, whose proof can be found in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
X
be a non-negative integer-valued random variable. Define the characteristic function
\varphi(t)=\mathbbm{E}[e^{itX}]
. Consider the following two sets of assumptions:

1.

For some
c\in\mathbbm{C}\setminus i\mathbbm{R}
and
\nu\in(0,1)
,

\varphi(t)=1+(c\mathbbm{1}_{(t>0)}+\overline{c}\mathbbm{1}_{(t<0)})|t|^{\nu}+o(t^{\nu}),\quad\text{as $t\to 0$}.

(6.3)

2.

For some
c_{1}>0
,
c_{2}\in\mathbbm{C}\setminus\mathbbm{R}
, and
\nu\in(0,1)
,

\varphi(t)=1+ic_{1}t+(c_{2}\mathbbm{1}_{(t>0)}+\overline{c}_{2}\mathbbm{1}_{(t<0)})|t|^{1+\nu}+o(t^{1+\nu}),\quad\text{as $t\to 0$}.

(6.4)

Then:

1.

In the case of Assumption
1
,
\operatorname{Re}(c)<0
and
\operatorname{Im}(c)>0
, and setting
a=-\frac{\operatorname{Re}(c)}{\Gamma(1-\nu)}\sec\Big(\tfrac{\pi\nu}{2}\Big)>0
,

\mathbbm{P}[X>R]=aR^{-\nu}+o(R^{-\nu}),\quad\text{as $R\to\infty$}.

(6.5)

2.

In the case of Assumption
2
,
\operatorname{Im}(c_{2})<0
and setting
a=-\frac{\nu\operatorname{Im}(c_{2})}{\Gamma(1-\nu)}\sec\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tfrac{\pi\nu}{2}}}\right)>0
,

\mathbbm{P}[X>R]=aR^{-\nu-1}+o(R^{-\nu-1}),\quad\text{as $R\to\infty$}.

(6.6)

6.1
The LDP case

We start by looking at the case when
{\operatorname{XDP}}=\operatorname{LDP}
. Thanks to Items
(iii)
and
(iv)
of Theorem
1.7
, we know that
f
is a symmetric distribution on
\mathbbm{Z}
, and
g
is a distribution on
\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq-1}
.
We introduce the three characteristic functions

F(t):=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}f(x)\,e^{itx},\qquad F^{-}(t):=\sum_{x\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq-1}}f(x)\,e^{itx},\qquad G(s):=\sum_{y\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq-1}}g(y)\,e^{isy}.

(6.7)

By the symmetry of
f
, we have
F(t)\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, since every characteristic function
\varphi(t)
satisfies
\varphi(-t)=\overline{\varphi(t)}
, in what follows, we only focus on the case
t\geq 0
.

Proposition 6.2

(Asymptotics of the characteristic functions, LDP case)
.

Setting

\kappa:=3+f(0)>0,

it holds that as
t\downarrow 0
,

\displaystyle F(t)=1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}),

\displaystyle F^{-}(t)=\frac{1}{2}(1-f(0))+\frac{-1-i\sqrt{3}}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}),

\displaystyle G(t)=1+\frac{-1-i\sqrt{3}}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}).

Before proving Proposition
6.2
, we note that it immediately implies Item
(va)
of Theorem
1.8
.

Proof of Item
(va)
of Theorem
1.8
(assuming Proposition
6.2
).

We combine Proposition
6.2
with the Tauberian result in Proposition
6.1
. The asymptotic expansion of
F(t)
in Proposition
6.2
ensures that
f(0)\neq 1
. Recalling the notation in (
6.2
), in the case of
f
, we apply Proposition
6.1
to the random variable
X
with
\mathbbm{P}[X=x]=\frac{2}{1-f(0)}f(x)
for all
x\geq 1
, so that
X
is non-negative (recall that
f(x)=f(-x)
). Since the characteristic function of
X
for
t>0
is

\displaystyle\varphi_{X}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1-f(0)}(F(t)-F^{-}(t)-f(0))

\displaystyle=1+\frac{-1+i\sqrt{3}}{1-f(0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}),

we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}[X>R]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-f(0)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-2/3)}\sec\Big(\tfrac{\pi}{3}\Big)R^{-2/3}+o(R^{-2/3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\Gamma(2/3)}{\pi(1-f(0))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}R^{-2/3}+o(R^{-2/3})

Hence, recalling that
f(x)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)=x}}\right]
for all
x\in\mathbbm{Z}
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)|>R}}\right]=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\Gamma(2/3)}{\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}R^{-2/3}+o(R^{-2/3}).

(6.8)

In the case of
g
, we apply Proposition
6.1
to the random variable
Y
with
\mathbbm{P}[Y=y]=g(-y)
for all
y\geq 1
, so that
Y
is non-negative.
Since the characteristic function of
Y
for
t>0
is

\varphi_{Y}(t)=G(-t)=\overline{G(t)}=1+\frac{-1+i\sqrt{3}}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3})

we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}[Y>R]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-2/3)}\sec\Big(\tfrac{\pi}{3}\Big)R^{-2/3}+o(R^{-2/3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\Gamma(2/3)}{2\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}R^{-2/3}+o(R^{-2/3})

Hence, recalling that
g(y)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)=y}}\right]
for all
y\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq-1}
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)<-R}}\right]=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\Gamma(2/3)}{2\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}R^{-2/3}+o(R^{-2/3}).

(6.9)

Setting
c_{1}=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\Gamma(2/3)}{2\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}
in (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) gives the expressions in the statement of the theorem.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Note that we do not know the exact value of
c_{1}=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\Gamma(2/3)}{2\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3+f(0)}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}
because we do not know the value of
f(0)
.

To prove Proposition
6.2
, we first express
\mathcal{X}
in terms of the Busemann function
\mathcal{X}^{1}
of
M_{1,\infty}
.

Lemma 6.4

(Express
\mathcal{X}
in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
in the LDP case)
.

Let
{\operatorname{XDP}}=\operatorname{LDP}
. Define the KMSW encoding walk
\mathcal{Z}:\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
, the infinite directed triangulations
M_{0,\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{1,\infty}
, and their associated Busemann functions
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{1}
as at the beginning of Section
6
.

•

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(1,-1)
, then

\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)-\max\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}\,,\,-\max\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}}}\right).

(6.10)

•

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(-1,0)
, then

\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-1\,,\,-1}}\right).

(6.11)

•

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(0,1)
, then

\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)}}\right).

(6.12)

Figure 15:

A schema for the proof of Lemmas
6.4
and
6.10
. The left-hand side is for the LDP case, while the right-hand side is for the SDP case. The three rows correspond to the three different steps
\{(1,-1),(-1,0),(0,1)\}
the walk
\mathcal{Z}
can take between time 0 and 1. The violet edges correspond to the root edges of the maps, the red vertex is the vertex
x^{0}_{0}
(resp.
x^{1}_{0}
for the map
M_{1,\infty}
), with the vertices
(x^{0}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
(resp.
(x^{1}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
) on the right and the vertices
(x^{0}_{i})_{i\leq 1}
(resp.
(x^{1}_{i})_{i\leq 1}
) on the left. On the maps
M_{1,\infty}
, we show some of the values of the Busemann function
\mathcal{X}^{1}
below each vertex. On the maps
M_{0,\infty}
, we show some of the values of the shifted Busemann function
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
of (
6.14
) in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
. We have
\mathcal{X}(\cdot)=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(\cdot)-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
.

Proof.

Recall that
M_{0,\infty}
and
M_{1,\infty}
are constructed via the KMSW procedure from
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\infty}
and
\mathcal{Z}|_{[1,\infty)}
, respectively.
We label the boundary vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
from left to right by
\{x^{0}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
so that
\lambda_{0}=(x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
and the boundary vertices of
M_{1,\infty}
from left to right by
\{x^{1}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
so that
\lambda_{1}=(x_{0}^{1},x_{1}^{1})
.
By Theorem
1.4
(applied to, e.g., a sequence of vertices along the right boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
), there exists
w\in\mathcal{V}(M_{1,\infty})
such that

\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{j,\infty}}(x^{j}_{k^{\prime}},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{j,\infty}}(x^{j}_{k},w)=\mathcal{X}^{j}(k^{\prime})-\mathcal{X}^{j}(k),\quad\forall j\in\{0,1\},\quad\forall k,k^{\prime}\in[-2,2]\cap\mathbbm{Z}.

(6.13)

We will analyze how the Busemann function changes when we perform one backward step of the KMSW procedure (Definition
3.3
) to get
M_{0,\infty}
from
M_{1,\infty}
.
Since
\mathcal{X}^{1}
is normalized so that
\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
, to facilitate the comparison, it is convenient to introduce

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(k):=\mathcal{X}(k)+\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x^{0}_{0},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{1,\infty}}(x^{1}_{0},w)=\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x^{0}_{k},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{1,\infty}}(x^{1}_{0},w).

(6.14)

Then
\mathcal{X}(\cdot)=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(\cdot)-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
. We will express
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)
in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
, then subtract
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
from
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)
to get an expression for
\mathcal{X}(-1)
and
\mathcal{X}(1)
in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
.

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(1,-1)
, then the edges on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
are the same as the edges on the boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
, with the only difference being that the root edge
\lambda_{0}
of
M_{0,\infty}
is the missing edge to the left of the root edge
\lambda_{1}
of
M_{1,\infty}
; see the top-left diagram in
Figure

15
.
In this case, an LDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from the vertex
x^{0}_{-1}=x^{1}_{-2}
to
w
must be an LDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from the vertex
x^{1}_{-2}
to
w
because the vertex
x^{0}_{0}
has only outgoing edges in
M_{0,\infty}
thanks to the last claim in Lemma
3.8
. Hence, in the notation (
6.14
),

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2).

An LDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{0}^{0}=x_{-1}^{1}
to
w
must be the longest between an LDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{-1}
to
w
and an LDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{0}
to
w
with the additional edge
\lambda_{0}
prepended. Recalling that
\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
, this gives

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1}}\right\}.

An LDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{1}^{0}=x_{0}^{1}
cannot use the edge
\lambda_{0}
(which is incoming to
x_{0}^{0}
), so
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
.
Writing
\mathcal{X}(-1)=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
and
\mathcal{X}(1)=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
and plugging in the above relations gives (
6.10
).

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(-1,0)
, then the edges on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
are the same as the edges on the boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
, with the only difference being that the root edge
\lambda_{1}
of
M_{1,\infty}
is replaced in the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
by a path of length two with the following properties. The first edge in the path is a missing edge whose initial vertex coincides with the initial vertex of
\lambda_{1}
. The second edge in the path is the new root edge
\lambda_{0}
whose terminal vertex coincides with the terminal vertex of
\lambda_{1}
; see the middle-left diagram in
Figure

15
. From this, we obtain

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0,\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)

where in the case of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
we used that an LDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{0}^{0}
to
w
must be an LDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{1}
to
w
, with the additional edge
\lambda_{0}
prepended. Subtracting gives (
6.11
).

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(0,1)
, then the edges on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
are the same as the edges on the boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
, with the only difference being that the root edge
\lambda_{1}
of
M_{1,\infty}
and the edge
e
immediately to the right of it are replaced in the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
by the new root edge
\lambda_{0}
whose initial vertex coincides with the initial vertex of
\lambda_{1}
and whose terminal vertex coincides with the terminal vertex of
e
; see the bottom-left diagram in
Figure

15
.
This leads to

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0,\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(2),

where here we used that there is a directed path of length two in
M_{1,\infty}
between the two vertices of
\lambda_{0}
, so no LDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
can traverse
\lambda_{0}
. Subtracting gives (
6.12
).
∎

Lemma
6.4
immediately implies the following recursive equation for the distributions
f
and
g
.

Proposition 6.5

(LDP recursive equation)
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,

3f(x)g(y)=f(x)f(y)+f(x)\sum_{j=1}^{-y-1}g(y+j)g(-j)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}f(j+x+1)f(-j)+g(-x-1)}}\right).

(6.15)

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
. Using the fact that
\mathcal{X}^{1}\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}
(which follows from the fact that
M_{0,\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{1,\infty}
), we can write

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Big(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)\Big)=(x,y)}}\right]=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=(x,y)}}\right].

Thanks to Lemma
6.4
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=(x,y)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)-\max\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}\,,\,-\max\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}}}\right)=(x,y)}}\right]

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1}{3}\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-1\,,\,-1}}\right)=(x,y)}}\right]

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1}{3}\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)}}\right)=(x,y)}}\right].

(6.16)

By stationarity (recall (
6.1
) and (
6.2
)) and the fact that
\mathcal{X}(0)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
,

f(x)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\mathcal{X}(-1)=x}}\right]=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=x}}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad g(y)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)=y}}\right]=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=y}}\right].

Furthermore, the increments
\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
are independent, the increments
\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)\}_{k\geq 1}
all have the same distribution, and the increments
\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)\}_{k\leq 0}
all have the same distribution (Properties
(i)
and
(ii)
in Theorem
1.7
). Applying these facts in (
6.1
) gives

\displaystyle 3f(-x)g(y)

\displaystyle=f(-x)f(y)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}\sum_{j\leq 0}f(j-x-1)f(-j)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}\,\,g(-x-1)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,+f(-x)\sum_{j=1}^{-y-1}g(y+j)g(-j),

where the first probability on the right-hand side of (
6.1
) involving the maximum was computed by distinguishing the cases when
\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\leq 0
and
\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\geq 1
. Using the fact that
f(x)=f(-x)
, we obtain the expression stated in the proposition.
∎

We now proceed with the proof of Proposition
6.2
by first establishing some preliminary results.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
Y{\sim}f
. Then
Y
is not a.s. constant and
\mathbbm{P}(Y=0)=f(0)>0
.

Proof.

We first prove that
Y
is not a.s. constant. Since
f(x)=f(-x)
, it is enough to show that
f(0)=1
is not a solution to (
6.15
).
Assume for contradiction that
f(0)=1
. Then
f(k)=0
for all
k\neq 0
.
Fixing
x=0
in (
6.15
), we obtain that for all
y\leq-1
,

3g(y)=f(y)+\sum_{j=1}^{-y-1}g(y+j)g(-j)+\mathbf{1}_{\{y=-1\}}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}f(j+1)f(-j)+g(-1)}}\right).

(6.17)

First, take
y=-1
. Since
f(-1)=0
and
f(j+1)=0
for all
j\geq 0
, (
6.17
) gives
3\,g(-1)=g(-1)
, and so
g(-1)=0
.
Now let
y\leq-2
. The indicator term vanishes and
f(y)=0
since
y\neq 0
, so (
6.17
) becomes

3g(y)=\sum_{j=1}^{-y-1}g(y+j)g(-j).

(6.18)

For
y=-2
, the right-hand side is
g(-1)g(-1)=0
, hence
g(-2)=0
.
Inductively, one obtains that
g(y)=0
for all
y\leq-1
. A contradiction since
g
is a probability distribution supported on
\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq-1}
.

We now prove that
f(0)>0
. Fixing
y=-1
and summing (
6.15
) over all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get using the symmetry of
f
,

3g(-1)=f(-1)+\frac{1+f(0)}{2}+1,

(6.19)

and so
g(-1)\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
Now take
x=0
,
y=-1
in (
6.15
):

3f(0)g(-1)=f(0)f(-1)+\sum_{j\geq 0}f(j+1)f(j)+g(-1),

again using the symmetry of
f
. If
f(0)=0
, this gives
g(-1)\leq 0
, resulting in a contradiction.
∎

To simplify computations, we introduce the auxiliary characteristic function:

\overline{G}(s):=\sum_{y\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 1}}g(-y)\,e^{isy},

(6.20)

that is,
\overline{G}(-s)=G(s)
, where
G(s)
was introduced in (
6.7
).
Multiplying (
6.15
) by
e^{i(tx-sy)}
and summing over
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
y\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq-1}
, we obtain the functional equation

3\overline{G}(s)F(t)=F(t)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F^{-}(-s)+\overline{G}(s)^{2}}}\right)+e^{is-it}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F(t)\Big(F^{-}(t)+f(0)\Big)+\overline{G}(t)}}\right).

(6.21)

Setting
s=0
in (
6.21
) and using
F^{-}(0)=\frac{1}{2}(1-f(0))
and
\overline{G}(0)=1
, gives

3F(t)=F(t)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tfrac{3-f(0)}{2}}}\right)+e^{-it}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F(t)\Big(F^{-}(t)+f(0)\Big)+\overline{G}(t)}}\right).

(6.22)

Solving (
6.22
) for
\overline{G}(t)
yields

\overline{G}(t)=-\,F(t)\,\widehat{F}^{-}(t),\qquad\widehat{F}^{-}(t):=F^{-}(t)+c(t),\qquad c(t):=f(0)-\frac{\kappa}{2}\,e^{it},

(6.23)

where we recall that
\kappa=3+f(0)>0
.
Now set
t=0
in (
6.21
) and substitute the expression for
\overline{G}(t)
in (
6.23
). After some algebra, we obtain the quadratic equation
16
16

16

Just performing the substitution, one gets a minus sign on the left-hand side of (
6.24
) that we removed for simplicity.
in the variable
\widehat{F}^{-}
:

F^{2}\big(\widehat{F}^{-}\big)^{2}+(3F-1)\,\widehat{F}^{-}+F=0.

(6.24)

Lemma 6.7

(
\widehat{F}^{-}(t)
has non-trivial imaginary part)
.

Let
\Delta(F):=(3F-1)^{2}-4F^{3}
be the discriminant of (
6.24
). Then there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
t
with
0<t<\delta
,

\Delta(F(t))<0,

hence the two roots of (
6.24
) are non-real complex conjugates. In particular, writing

\widehat{F}^{-}(t)=\alpha(t)+i\beta(t)

(6.25)

with a continuous choice of branch, one has
\beta(t)\neq 0
for
0<t<\delta
.

Proof.

We have
F(0)=1
,
F
is continuous, and
\Delta(1)=\Delta^{\prime}(1)=0
,
\Delta^{\prime\prime}(1)=-6<0
. Thus

\Delta(1+\varepsilon)\leq-C\,\varepsilon^{2}

(6.26)

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon\in\mathbbm{R}\setminus\{0\}
and some
C>0
. Let
Y{\sim}f
and
d
be the lattice span of
Y
, i.e.

d:=\gcd\{x-y:f(x)>0,\ f(y)>0\}.

Since
Y
is not a.s. constant by Lemma
6.6
, we have
d<\infty
. Since
f(0)>0
thanks to Lemma
6.6
, a simple argument for the characteristic function of integer-valued random variables gives

F(t)=1\quad\text{if and only if}\quad t\in\tfrac{2\pi}{d}\mathbb{Z}.

(6.27)

So there exists
\delta_{1}\in(0,2\pi/d)
such that

F(t)\neq 1
for all
0<t<\delta_{1}
. By continuity, there exists
\delta_{2}>0
such that

|F(t)-1|<\eta
whenever
t<\delta_{2}
, with
\eta>0
small enough to ensure the estimate in (
6.26
) applies.
Setting
\delta:=\min\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}
, we get that for all
0<t<\delta
,

F(t)\neq 1,\qquad\Delta(F(t))\leq-C(F(t)-1)^{2}<0.

Thus
\Delta(F(t))<0
for
0<|t|<\delta
, so the two roots of (
6.24
) are non-real conjugates with nonzero imaginary part.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of Proposition
6.2
.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

Recall that
F(t)\in\mathbb{R}
and write

F(t)=f(0)+F^{-}(t)+\overline{F^{-}(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Ghat}}}{{=}}f(0)+(\widehat{F}^{-}(t)-c(t))+\overline{(\widehat{F}^{-}(t)-c(t))}=f(0)-2\operatorname{Re}(c(t))+2\operatorname{Re}(\widehat{F}^{-}(t)).

We set

K(t):=f(0)-2\operatorname{Re}(c(t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Ghat}}}{{=}}-f(0)+\kappa\,\cos t,

so that
K(t)
encodes the part of
F(t)
that is independent of
\widehat{F}^{-}(t)
(which we recall is our indeterminate in (
6.24
)) and

\operatorname{Re}(\widehat{F}^{-}(t))=\frac{F(t)-K(t)}{2}.

(6.28)

Hence, thanks to Lemma
6.7
,

\widehat{F}^{-}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:fhatexpr2}}}{{=}}\alpha(t)+i\beta(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:real-part-fhat}}}{{=}}\frac{F(t)-K(t)}{2}+i\beta(t),

with
\beta(t)\neq 0
for
0<t<\delta
.

Now, taking the imaginary part in (
6.24
), we obtain the equation

\beta(F^{3}-K\,F^{2}+3F-1)=0,

and since
\beta(t)\neq 0
for
0<t<\delta
, we get that for
0<t<\delta
,

F^{3}-K\,F^{2}+3F-1=0.

(6.29)

The Taylor expansion of
K(t)
around
t=0
is

K(t)=3-\frac{\kappa}{2}\,t^{2}+O(t^{4}).

Writing
F(t)=1+\varepsilon(t)
with
\varepsilon(t)\to 0
as
t\downarrow 0
, we get from (
6.29
) that

0=\varepsilon^{3}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\,t^{2}(\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon+1)+O\!\big(t^{4}\big).

(6.30)

Now, since
\kappa>0
(and so
\kappa\neq 0
), we claim that as
t\downarrow 0
,

\varepsilon(t)=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}).

(6.31)

Indeed, set
\varepsilon(t)=t^{2/3}s(t)
. Dividing (
6.30
) by
t^{2}
gives as
t\downarrow 0
,

0=s^{3}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\bigl(1+2st^{2/3}+s^{2}t^{4/3}\bigr)+O(t^{2})=s^{3}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}+o(1),

(6.32)

where in the last equality we used that
\varepsilon(t)=t^{2/3}s(t)\to 0
as
t\downarrow 0
. Hence
s(t)=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}+o(1)
and so (
6.31
) holds.
This completes the proof for the expansion of
F(t)
.

We now determine the expansions of
F^{-}(t)
and
\overline{G}(t)
. Solving the quadratic equation (
6.24
), the two possible continuous choices of branch for
\widehat{F}^{-}(t)
are

\widehat{F}_{\pm}^{-}(t)=\frac{1-3F\pm\sqrt{\Delta(F)}}{2F^{2}},\qquad{\text{with $\Delta(F)=(3F-1)^{2}-4F^{3}$,}}

and recalling (
6.23
), we obtain that the two possible continuous choices of branch for
F^{-}(t)
are

F_{\pm}^{-}(t)=\frac{1-3F\pm\sqrt{\Delta(F)}}{2F^{2}}-c(t),\qquad{\text{with $\Delta(F)=(3F-1)^{2}-4F^{3}.$}}

(6.33)

Moreover, from (
6.23
), we have that the two possible continuous choices of branch for
\overline{G}(t)
are

\overline{G}_{\pm}(t)=-F(t)\,\widehat{F}^{-}_{\pm}(t).

(6.34)

Therefore, since
F(t)=1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3})
as
t\downarrow 0
, we get from (
6.23
) and (
6.33
) that

F_{\pm}^{-}(t)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-f(0)}{2}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}(-1\pm i\sqrt{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}),

and from (
6.34
),

\overline{G}_{\pm}(t)=1+\frac{1}{2}(-1\mp i\sqrt{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}).

Noting that
\overline{G}(t)
, introduced in (
6.20
), is the characteristic function of a non-negative integer-valued random variable, and using Proposition
6.1
, we have that the only admissible expression is

\overline{G}(t)=1+\frac{1}{2}(-1+i\sqrt{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}),

and so

F^{-}(t)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-f(0)}{2}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}(-1-i\sqrt{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}).

This completes the proof for the expansion of
F^{-}(t)
and
\overline{G}(t)
as
t\downarrow 0
. To recover the expansion of
G(t)
, it is enough to note that
G(t)=\overline{G}(-t)
and the latter is just the complex conjugate of
\overline{G}(t)
.
∎

6.2
The SDP case

We now move to the case when
{\operatorname{XDP}}=\operatorname{SDP}
.
The proof is very similar to the LDP case.
We use the same notation as in Section
6.1
, but now everything is defined with
\operatorname{SDP}
instead of
\operatorname{LDP}
.

Thanks to Properties
(iii)
and
(iv)
of Theorem
1.7
we know that
f
is a symmetric distribution on
\mathbbm{Z}
and
g
is a distribution on
\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq-1}
.
We introduce the three characteristic functions

F(t):=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}f(x)\,e^{itx},\qquad F^{-}(t):=\sum_{x\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq-1}}f(x)\,e^{itx},\qquad G(s):=\sum_{y\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq-1}}g(y)\,e^{isy}.

(6.35)

By the symmetry of
f
, we note that
F(t)\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, since every characteristic function
\varphi(t)
satisfies
\varphi(-t)=\overline{\varphi(t)}
, in what follows, we only focus on the case
t\geq 0
.

Proposition 6.8

(Asymptotics of the characteristic functions, SDP case)
.

Setting

\kappa:=3-6\,g(-1)^{2}-f(0),

it holds that
\kappa=0
, and, as
t\downarrow 0
,

\displaystyle F(t)=1-g(-1)^{2/3}\,t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3}),

\displaystyle F^{-}(t)=\frac{1}{2}(1-f(0))-2i\,g(-1)^{2}\,t+\frac{-1+i\sqrt{3}}{2}g(-1)^{2/3}\,t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3}),

\displaystyle G(t)=1+\frac{-1-i\sqrt{3}}{2}g(-1)^{2/3}\,t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3}).

Moreover,
g(-1)>0
.

Before proving Proposition
6.8
, we note that it immediately implies Item
(vb)
of Theorem
1.8
.

Proof of Item
(vb)
of Theorem
1.8
(assuming Proposition
6.8
).

We first combine Proposition
6.8
with Proposition
6.1
to prove the claimed tail behaviors. The asymptotic expansions of
F(t)
and
G(t)
in Proposition
6.8
ensures that
f(0)\neq 1
and
g(-1)\neq 1
. Moreover,
g(-1)\neq 0
as stated in Proposition
6.8
.
Recalling the notation introduced in (
6.2
), in the case of
f
, we apply Proposition
6.1
to the random variable
X
with
\mathbbm{P}[X=x]=\frac{2}{1-f(0)}f(x)
for all
x\geq 1
, so that
X
is non-negative (recall that
f(x)=f(-x)
). Since the characteristic function of
X
for
t>0
is

\displaystyle\varphi_{X}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1-f(0)}\Big(F(t)-F^{-}(t)-f(0)\Big)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{4i\,g(-1)^{2}}{1-f(0)}\,t+\frac{(-1-i\sqrt{3})\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{1-f(0)}\,t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3}),

we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}[X>R]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{1-f(0)}\frac{4}{3\,\Gamma(1-4/3)}\sec\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tfrac{2\pi}{3}}}\right)R^{-4/3}+o(R^{-4/3})

\displaystyle=\frac{4\,\Gamma(1/3)\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{3\pi(1-f(0))}\,R^{-4/3}+o(R^{-4/3}).

Hence, recalling that
f(x)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)=x}}\right]
for all
x\in\mathbbm{Z}
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)|>R}}\right]=\frac{4\,\Gamma(1/3)\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{3\pi}\,R^{-4/3}+o(R^{-4/3}).

(6.36)

In the case of
g
, we apply Proposition
6.1
to the random variable
Y
with
\mathbbm{P}[Y=y]=\frac{1}{1-g(-1)}g(y)
for all
y\geq 0
, so that
Y
is non-negative.
Since the characteristic function of
Y
for
t>0
is

\varphi_{Y}(t)=\frac{1}{1-g(-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G(t)-g(-1)e^{-it}}}\right)=1+\frac{i\,g(-1)}{1-g(-1)}\,t+\frac{g(-1)^{2/3}(-1-i\sqrt{3})}{2(1-g(-1))}\,t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3}),

where we used that
e^{-it}=1-it+O(t^{2})
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}[Y>R]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{2(1-g(-1))}\frac{4}{3\Gamma(1-4/3)}\sec\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tfrac{2\pi}{3}}}\right)R^{-4/3}+o(R^{-4/3})

\displaystyle=\frac{4\,\Gamma(1/3)\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{6\pi(1-g(-1))}R^{-4/3}+o(R^{-4/3})

Hence, recalling that
g(y)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)=y}}\right]
for all
y\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq-1}
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)>R}}\right]=\frac{4\,\Gamma(1/3)\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{6\pi}R^{-4/3}+o(R^{-4/3}).

(6.37)

Setting
c_{2}=\frac{4\,\Gamma(1/3)\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{6\pi}
in (
6.36
) and (
6.37
) gives the expressions in the statement of the theorem.

The tail expansions (
6.36
) and (
6.37
), together with the stationarity property
(ii)
of Theorem
1.7
, imply that
\mathbbm{E}[|\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)|]<\infty
for every
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
. From the expansions of
F(t)
and
G(t)
in Proposition
6.8
, we get
F^{\prime}(0)=G^{\prime}(0)=0
, which implies that
\mathbbm{E}[\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)]=0
for every
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

Also in this case, we do not know the exact value of
c_{2}=\frac{4\,\Gamma(1/3)\,g(-1)^{2/3}}{6\pi}
because we do not know the value of
g(-1)
.

As in Section
6.1
, the first step in the proof of Proposition
6.8
is to express
\mathcal{X}
in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
.

Lemma 6.10

(Express
\mathcal{X}
in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
in the SDP case)
.

Let
{\operatorname{XDP}}=\operatorname{SDP}
. Define the KMSW encoding walk
\mathcal{Z}:\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
and the infinite directed triangulations
M_{0,\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{1,\infty}
and their associated Busemann functions
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{1}
as at the beginning of Section
6
.

•

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(1,-1)
, then

\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}\,,\,-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}}}\right).

(6.38)

•

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(-1,0)
, then

\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-1\,,\,-1}}\right).

(6.39)

•

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(0,1)
, then

\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)-\min\{0,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)+1\}\,,\,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)-\min\{0,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)+1\}}}\right).

(6.40)

Proof.

Recall that
M_{0,\infty}
and
M_{1,\infty}
are constructed via the KPZ procedure from
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\infty)}
and
\mathcal{Z}|_{[1,\infty)}
, respectively.
We label the boundary vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
from left to right by
\{x^{0}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
so that
\lambda_{0}=(x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
and the boundary vertices of
M_{1,\infty}
from left to right by
\{x^{1}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
so that
\lambda_{1}=(x_{0}^{1},x_{1}^{1})
.
By Theorem
1.4
(applied to, e.g., a sequence of vertices along the right boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
), there exists
w\in\mathcal{V}(M_{1,\infty})
such that

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{j,\infty}}(x^{j}_{k^{\prime}},w)-\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{j,\infty}}(x^{j}_{k},w)=\mathcal{X}^{j}(k^{\prime})-\mathcal{X}^{j}(k),\quad\forall j\in\{0,1\},\quad\forall k,k^{\prime}\in[-2,2]\cap\mathbbm{Z}.

(6.41)

We analyze how the Busemann function changes when we perform one backward step of the KMSW procedure (Definition
3.3
), to get
M_{0,\infty}
from
M_{1,\infty}
. Exactly as in the LDP case (
6.14
), we introduce

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(k):=\mathcal{X}(k)+\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x^{0}_{0},w)-\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{1,\infty}}(x^{1}_{0},w)=\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x^{0}_{k},w)-\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{1,\infty}}(x^{1}_{0},w).

(6.42)

Then
\mathcal{X}(\cdot)=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(\cdot)-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
. We will express
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1),\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)
in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
, then subtract
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
from
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)
to get an expression for
\mathcal{X}(-1)
and
\mathcal{X}(1)
in terms of
\mathcal{X}^{1}
.

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(1,-1)
, then the edges on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
are the same as the edges on the boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
, with the only difference being that the root edge
\lambda_{0}
of
M_{0,\infty}
is the missing edge to the left of the root edge
\lambda_{1}
of
M_{1,\infty}
; see the top-right diagram in
Figure

15
.
In this case, an SDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from the vertex
x^{0}_{-1}=x_{-2}^{1}
to
w
must be an SDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from the vertex
x^{1}_{-2}
to
w
because the vertex
x^{0}_{0}
has only outgoing edges in
M_{0,\infty}
thanks to the last claim in Lemma
3.8
. Hence, in the notation (
6.42
),

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2).

An SDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{0}^{0}=x_{-1}^{1}
to
w
must be the shortest between an SDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{-1}
to
w
and an SDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{0}
to
w
with the additional edge
\lambda_{0}
prepended. Recalling that
\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
, this gives

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1}}\right\}.

Since the edge
\lambda_{0}
is incoming to
x_{1}^{0}=x_{0}^{0}
, we also have
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
.
Subtracting now gives (
6.38
).

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(-1,0)
, then the edges on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
are the same as the edges on the boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
, with the only difference being that the root edge
\lambda_{1}
of
M_{1,\infty}
is replaced in the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
by a path of length two with the following properties. The first edge in the path is a missing edge whose initial vertex coincides with the initial vertex of
\lambda_{1}
. The second edge in the path is the new root edge
\lambda_{0}
whose terminal vertex coincides with the terminal vertex of
\lambda_{1}
; see the middle-right diagram in
Figure

15
. In this case,

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0,\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(1),

where for
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)
we used that an SDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{0}^{0}
to
w
must be an SDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{1}
to
w
with the additional edge
\lambda_{0}
prepended. Subtracting gives (
6.39
).

If
\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(0,1)
, then the edges on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
are the same as the edges on the boundary of
M_{1,\infty}
, with the only difference being that the root edge
\lambda_{1}
of
M_{1,\infty}
and the edge
e
immediately to the right of it are replaced in the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
by the new root edge
\lambda_{0}
whose initial vertex coincides with the initial vertex of
\lambda_{1}
and whose terminal vertex coincides with the terminal vertex of
e
; see the bottom-right diagram in
Figure

15
. In this case, an SDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from the vertex
x^{0}_{-1}=x_{-1}^{1}
to
w
must be an SDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from the vertex
x^{1}_{-1}
to
w
because the vertex
x^{0}_{0}
has only outgoing edges in
M_{0,\infty}
thanks to the last claim in Lemma
3.8
. Hence,

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(-1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1).

An SDP geodesic in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{0}^{0}=x_{0}^{1}
to
w
must be the shortest between an SDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{0}
to
w
and an SDP geodesic in
M_{1,\infty}
from
x^{1}_{2}
to
w
with the additional edge
\lambda_{0}
prepended. Recalling that
\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
, this gives

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(0)=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)+1}}\right\}.

Since
\lambda_{0}
is incoming to
x_{1}^{0}=x_{2}^{1}
, we also have
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)
. Subtracting gives (
6.40
).
∎

Lemma
6.10
immediately implies the following recursive equation for the distributions
f
and
g
.

Proposition 6.11

(SDP recursive equation)
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
.

\;3\,f(x)g(y)=f(x)\Bigg(f(y)+\sum_{j=-1}^{y+1}g(j)g\big(y-j\big)\Bigg)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}\Bigg(\sum_{j\leq-2}f(j)f\big(x-j-1\big)+g(-1)^{2}f(x+1)+g(x-1)\Bigg).\;

(6.43)

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
. Using the fact that
\mathcal{X}^{1}\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}
(which follows from the fact that
M_{0,\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{1,\infty}
), we can write

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Big(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)\Big)=(x,y)}}\right]=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=(x,y)}}\right].

Thanks to Lemma
6.10
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1)\,,\,\mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=(x,y)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}\,,\,-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}}}\right]=(x,y)}}\right)

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1}{3}\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-1\,,\,-1}}\right)=(x,y)}}\right]

(6.44)

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1}{3}\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)-\min\{0,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)+1\}\,,\,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)-\min\{0,\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)+1\}}}\right)=(x,y)}}\right].

By stationarity (recall (
6.1
) and (
6.2
)) and the fact that
\mathcal{X}(0)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)=0
,

f(x)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\mathcal{X}(-1)=x}}\right]=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=x}}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad g(y)=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(1)=y}}\right]=\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=y}}\right].

Furthermore, the increments
\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
are independent, the increments
\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)\}_{k\geq 1}
all have the same distribution, and the increments
\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)\}_{k\leq 0}
all have the same distribution (thanks to Items
(i)
and
(ii)
in Theorem
1.7
). Applying these facts in (
6.2
) gives

\displaystyle 3f(-x)g(y)

\displaystyle=f(-x)f(y)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}\sum_{j\leq-2}f(-x-j-1)f(j)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}g(-x-1)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,+f(-x)\sum_{j=-1}^{y+1}g(j)g\big(y-j\big)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}g(-1)^{2}f(-x+1),

where the first probability on the right-hand side of (
6.2
) involving the minimum was computed by distinguishing the cases when
\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\leq 0
and
\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)\geq 1
, and the second probability involving the minimum was computed by distinguishing the cases when
\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)\geq-1
and
\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)=-2
(since we must have that
\mathcal{X}^{1}(2)\geq-2
because
g
is supported on
\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq-1}
). Changing
-x
to
x
, we obtain the expression in the statement of the lemma.
∎

We now proceed with the proof of Proposition
6.8
by first establishing some preliminary results.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
Y{\sim}f
. Then
Y
is not a.s. constant and
\mathbbm{P}(Y=0)=f(0)>0
. Moreover,
g(-1)>0
.

Proof.

We first prove that
Y
is not a.s. constant. Since
f(x)=f(-x)
, it is enough to prove that
f(0)<1
. Assume for contradiction that
f(0)=1
.
Setting
x=0
in (
6.43
) gives, for all
y\geq-1
,

3g(y)=f(y)+\sum_{j=-1}^{y+1}g(j)g(y-j)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=-1\}}\,g(-1).

(6.45)

Take
y=-1
in (
6.45
). Noting
f(-1)=0
(since
f(0)=1
) and that the sum reduces to

2g(-1)g(0)
, we obtain

3g(-1)=2g(-1)g(0)+g(-1)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad g(-1)=g(-1)g(0).

Hence either
g(-1)=0
or
g(0)=1
.
If
g(0)=1
, taking
y=0
in (
6.45
) yields
3=1+1
, a contradiction;
thus
g(-1)=0
. Taking
y=0
in (
6.45
):

3g(0)=1+g(0)^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad g(0)=\frac{1}{2}(3-\sqrt{5})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

Next take
y=1
in (
6.45
). The only nonzero terms in the sum are

j=0,1
, so
3g(1)=2g(0)g(1)
, which forces
g(1)=0
because
g(0)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
.
An induction using (
6.45
) shows that for every
y\geq 2
the sum again
reduces to
2g(0)g(y)
and hence
g(y)=0
. Therefore

g(k)=0
for all
k\geq 1
, and since also
g(-1)=0
we have

\sum_{k}g(k)=g(0)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\neq 1
; a contradiction.

We now prove that
f(0)>0
and
g(-1)>0
.
Fixing
y=-1
in (
6.43
) and summing over
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, we obtain

3g(-1)=f(-1)+2g(-1)g(0)+\sum_{j\leq-2}f(j)+g(-1)^{2}+1.

(6.46)

By symmetry of
f
, we have that
f(-1)+\sum_{j\leq-2}f(j)=\frac{1-f(0)}{2}
,
so from (
6.46
),

3g(-1)=\frac{3-f(0)}{2}+2g(-1)g(0)+g(-1)^{2}\ \geq\ \frac{3-f(0)}{2}.

(6.47)

In particular,
g(-1)>0
. Now, evaluate (
6.43
) at
(x,y)=(0,-1)
:

3f(0)g(-1)=f(0)\big(f(-1)+2g(-1)g(0)\big)+\sum_{j\leq-2}f(j)f(-j-1)+g(-1)^{2}f(1)+g(-1)\geq g(-1).

If
f(0)=0
, the last equation gives a contradiction because
g(-1)>0
.
∎

Now, multiplying (
6.43
) by
e^{i(tx+sy)}
and summing over
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
y\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq-1}
, we get the equation

\displaystyle 3F(t)G(s)

\displaystyle=F(t)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F(s)-F^{-}(s)+f(-1)e^{-is}+G^{2}(s)-g(-1)^{2}e^{-2is}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+e^{-is}\Bigl[e^{it}F^{-}(t)F(t)-f(-1)F(t)+g(-1)^{2}F(t)e^{-it}+G(t)e^{it}\Bigr].

(6.48)

Setting
t=0
and using that
F(0)=1
,
F^{-}(0)=(1-f(0))/2
and
G(0)=1
, we get that

3G(s)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F(s)-F^{-}(s)+G^{2}(s)-g(-1)^{2}e^{-2is}}}\right]+e^{-is}\Bigl[(1-f(0))/2+g(-1)^{2}+1\Bigr].

(6.49)

Similarly, setting
s=0
, we get that

3F(t)=F(t)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[2-(1-f(0))/2-g(-1)^{2}}}\right]+\Bigl[e^{it}F^{-}(t)F(t)+g(-1)^{2}F(t)e^{-it}+G(t)e^{it}\Bigr].

This latter equation allows us to write
G(t)
in terms of
F(t)
and
F^{-}(t)
. More precisely, we have that

G(t)=-F(t)\,\widehat{F}^{-}(t)

(6.50)

where

\widehat{F}^{-}(t):=F^{-}(t)+c(t)

(6.51)

and

c(t):=\frac{2g(-1)^{2}+e^{it}(-3-2g(-1)^{2}+f(0))}{2e^{2it}}.

(6.52)

Substituting (
6.50
) in (
6.49
), one obtains the quadratic equation
17
17

17

Just performing the substitution, one gets a minus sign on the left-hand side of (
6.53
) that we removed for simplicity.
in
\widehat{F}^{-}
:

F^{2}(\widehat{F}^{-})^{2}+(3F-1)\widehat{F}^{-}+F=0.

(6.53)

Note that this is exactly the same equation that we obtained in (
6.24
) in the LDP case (but
c(t)
is defined differently).

We are now ready to complete the proof of Proposition
6.8
.

Proof of Proposition
6.8
.

The fact that
g(-1)>0
follows from Lemma
6.12
. We now determine the asymptotic expansions.
Recalling that
F(t)\in\mathbb{R}
and writing

F(t)=f(0)+F^{-}(t)+\overline{F^{-}(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:fhatmin}}}{{=}}f(0)+(\widehat{F}^{-}(t)-c(t))+\overline{(\widehat{F}^{-}(t)-c(t))}=f(0)-2\operatorname{Re}(c(t))+2\operatorname{Re}(\widehat{F}^{-}(t)),

we set

K(t):=f(0)-2\operatorname{Re}(c(t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:defn-ct}}}{{=}}f(0)+(3+2g(-1)^{2}-f(0))\cos(t)-2g(-1)^{2}\cos(2t),

so that
K(t)
encodes the part of
F(t)
that is independent of
\widehat{F}^{-}(t)
(which we recall is our indeterminate in (
6.53
)) and

\operatorname{Re}(\widehat{F}^{-}(t))=\frac{F(t)-K(t)}{2}.

(6.54)

Hence, thanks to Lemma
6.7
(which is valid also in this SDP case thanks to Lemma
6.12
),
we can write

\widehat{F}^{-}(t)=\alpha(t)+i\beta(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:real-part-fhat2}}}{{=}}\frac{F(t)-K(t)}{2}+i\beta(t),

and there exists
\delta>0
such that
\beta(t)\neq 0
for
0<t<\delta
.

Now, taking the imaginary part in (
6.53
), we obtain the equation

\beta(F^{3}-K\,F^{2}+3F-1)=0,

and since
\beta(t)\neq 0
for
0<t<\delta
, we get that for
0<t<\delta
,

F^{3}-K\,F^{2}+3F-1=0,

(6.55)

The Taylor expansion of
K(t)
around
t=0
is

K(t)=3-\frac{\kappa}{2}\,t^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{24}-g(-1)^{2}}}\right)\,t^{4}+O(t^{6}),

where we recall that
\kappa=3-6g(-1)^{2}-f(0)
. Writing
F(t)=1+\varepsilon(t)
with
\varepsilon(t)\to 0
as
t\downarrow 0
, we get from (
6.55
) that

0=\varepsilon^{3}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\,t^{2}(\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon+1)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{24}-g(-1)^{2}}}\right)\,t^{4}(\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon+1)+O(\varepsilon^{4}+t^{6}+t^{4}\varepsilon).

Now, to ensure that the right-hand side of the latter equation tends to zero as
t
decreases to zero:

•

If
\kappa=0
, we must have that
\varepsilon^{3}(1+o(1))=-g(-1)^{2}t^{4}(1+o(1))
as
t\downarrow 0
, and so
\varepsilon(t)=-g(-1)^{2/3}\,t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3})
as
t\downarrow 0
.

•

If
\kappa\neq 0
, we must have that
\varepsilon^{3}(1+o(1))=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\,t^{2}(1+o(1))
as
t\downarrow 0
. Since
F
is real,
F(0)=1
, and
|F(t)|\leq 1
for all
t\in\mathbbm{R}
, it must be that
\kappa>0
and so
\varepsilon(t)=-(\frac{\kappa}{2})^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3})
as
t\downarrow 0
.

The above two items complete the proof for the expansion of
F(t)
.

We now determine the expansions of
F^{-}(t)
and
G(t)
. Solving the quadratic equation (
6.53
), the two possible continuous choices of branch for
\widehat{F}^{-}(t)
are

\widehat{F}_{\pm}^{-}(t)=\frac{1-3F\pm\sqrt{\Delta(F)}}{2F^{2}},\qquad{\text{with $\Delta(F)=(3F-1)^{2}-4F^{3}$,}}

and recalling (
6.51
), we obtain that the two possible continuous choices of branch for
F^{-}(t)
are

F_{\pm}^{-}(t)=\frac{1-3F\pm\sqrt{\Delta(F)}}{2F^{2}}-c(t),\qquad{\text{with $\Delta(F)=(3F-1)^{2}-4F^{3}.$}}

(6.56)

Moreover, from (
6.50
), we have that the two possible continuous choices of branch for
G(t)
are

G_{\pm}(t)=-F(t)\,\widehat{F}^{-}_{\pm}(t).

(6.57)

Therefore,

•

If
\kappa=0
, since
F(t)=1-g(-1)^{2/3}\,t^{4/3}+o(|t|^{4/3})
as
t\downarrow 0
, we get from (
6.52
) and (
6.56
) that

F_{\pm}^{-}(t)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-f(0)}{2}}}\right)-2ig(-1)^{2}t+\frac{1}{2}(-1\pm i\sqrt{3})g(-1)^{2/3}t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3}),

and from (
6.57
),

G_{\pm}(t)=1+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-g(-1)^{2/3}\mp i\sqrt{3}g(-1)^{2/3}}}\right)t^{4/3}+o(t^{4/3}).

•

If
\kappa>0
, since
F(t)=1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}\,t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3})
as
t\downarrow 0
, we get from (
6.52
) and (
6.56
) that

F_{\pm}^{-}(t)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-f(0)}{2}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}(-1\pm i\sqrt{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3})

(6.58)

and from (
6.57
),

G_{\pm}(t)=1+\frac{1}{2}(-1\mp i\sqrt{3})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)^{1/3}t^{2/3}+o(t^{2/3}).

Noting that the conjugate of
F^{-}(t)
is the characteristic function of a non-negative integer-valued random variable, and using Proposition
6.1
, we have that the only admissible expressions when
\kappa=0
are the ones in the proposition statement.

Hence, it remains to prove that the case
\kappa>0
is not possible. This does not appear to follow from the recursive equation of Proposition
6.11
, and it is the only part of the proof that is not directly parallel to the LDP case. If
\kappa>0
, then the asymptotics in (
6.58
) combined with Proposition
6.1
give that as
x\to\infty
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)|\geq x}}\right]=c\,x^{-2/3}+o(x^{-2/3}),

(6.59)

for some
c>0
.

Recall from Theorem
1.4
that
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
is the sequence of boundary vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
, ordered from left to right so that
\lambda_{0}=(x_{0},x_{1})
.
The idea of the proof is to show that
\mathcal{X}(-k)
is bounded above by
O(k)
with high probability, which will violate the heavy-tailed central limit theorem if
\kappa>0
. We will do this using the triangle inequality and the fact that the rightmost directed path started from
x_{-k}
typically takes
O(k)
units of time to coalesce into the rightmost directed path started from
x_{1}
(i.e., the right boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
). See Figure
16
for an illustration.

Figure 16:

Illustration for the proof that
\kappa>0
is not admissible.

For
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
\tau_{-k}
be the smallest
n\in\mathbbm{N}
which
x_{-k}
is the initial vertex of
\lambda_{n}
, as in (
5.2
). By (
5.3
),
\tau_{-k}
can equivalently be defined as the smallest
n\in\mathbbm{N}
for which
\mathcal{L}(n)=-k
. By the convergence of the re-scaled encoding walk
k^{-1}\mathcal{Z}(\lfloor k^{2}\cdot\rfloor)
to a (correlated) two-dimensional Brownian motion, for each
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
A>1
such that for every
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, it holds with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\max_{n\in[0,\tau_{-k}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{R}(n)|\leq Ak.

(6.60)

Let
\eta_{k}
be the path on the boundary of
M_{\tau_{-k},\infty}
from
x_{-k}
to the point where the right boundaries of
M_{\tau_{-k},\infty}
and
M_{0,\infty}
meet. Equivalently,
\eta_{k}
consists of the edge
\lambda_{\tau_{-k}}
followed by the upper-right boundary of the submap of
M_{0,\infty}
obtained by applying the KMSW procedure to
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\tau_{-k}]}
. By Lemma
3.8
,
\eta_{k}
is a directed path in
M_{\tau_{-k},\infty}
started from
x_{k}
. By (
3.6
) of Lemma
3.6
, if (
6.60
) holds, then the length of
\eta_{k}
is at most
2Ak+1
. Hence

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|\eta_{k}|\leq 2Ak+1}}\right]\geq 1-\delta.

(6.61)

By Theorem
1.4
, there exists a vertex
w=w(k)\in\mathcal{V}(M_{\tau_{-k},\infty})
such that the SDP Busemann function satisfies

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{-k},w)-\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w)=\mathcal{X}(-k)-\mathcal{X}(0)=\mathcal{X}(-k).

(6.62)

Let
P
be an SDP geodesic from
x_{0}
to
w
in
M_{0,\infty}
. Since
\eta_{k}
is a path in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{-k}
to
x_{r}
for some
r\geq 0
, topological considerations imply that
P
must hit a vertex on
\eta_{k}
. Let
z
be such a vertex.
Then

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w)=\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},z)+\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(z,w).

(6.63)

On the other hand, concatenating the segment of
\eta_{k}
from
x_{-k}
to
z
with the segment of
P
from
z
to
w
gives a directed path from
x_{-k}
to
w
. Hence

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{-k},w)\leq|\eta_{k}|+\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(z,w).

(6.64)

We therefore obtain that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(-k)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{-k},w)-\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w)\quad

by (
6.62
)

\displaystyle\leq|\eta_{k}|-\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},z)

by (
6.63
) and (
6.64
)

\displaystyle\leq|\eta_{k}|\leq 2Ak+1

\displaystyle\text{by~\eqref{eqn-sdp-identify-bdy}}.

Since
\delta\in(0,1)
is arbitrary, any possible limit in law of the random variable
k^{-3/2}\mathcal{X}(-k)
is a.s. non-positive.
Since
\mathcal{X}(-k)\overset{d}{=}-\mathcal{X}(-k)
by Item
(iv)
of Theorem
1.7
, this implies that
k^{-3/2}\mathcal{X}(-k)
converges in probability to zero. By the heavy-tailed central limit theorem, this shows that the increments
\mathcal{X}(-k+1)-\mathcal{X}(-k)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)
for
k\leq 0
cannot have the tail asymptotic in (
6.59
). This shows that the case
\kappa>0
is not admissible, concluding the entire proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

This is immediate from Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
and the heavy-tailed functional central limit theorem (see, e.g.,
[
JS03
, Chapter VII]
). ∎

7
The case of finite triangulations

The goal of this section is to prove the finite-volume versions of our results, i.e. Theorems
1.11
–
1.13
.
We will first prove the statements for size-
n
KMSW cells (Theorems
1.12
and
1.13
), then use these to deduce the statement for Boltzmann bipolar-oriented triangulations (Theorem
1.11
).

7.1
LDP in size-
n
KMSW cells

Recall that
M_{-\infty,\infty}
denotes the UIBOT, the random walk
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}):\mathbbm{Z}\to\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
is its KMSW encoding walk as in Proposition
3.10
,
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
is the ordered sequence of boundary vertices of
M_{0,\infty}
(as in Theorem
1.4
), and
\tau_{k}
(introduced in (
5.2
)) is the smallest
n\in\mathbbm{N}
such that
x_{k}
is the initial vertex of the active edge
\lambda_{n}
.

For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
M_{0,n}
be the submap of
M_{0,\infty}
obtained by applying the KMSW procedure (Definition
3.3
) to
\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]}
, as in Section
1.3.2
. In this subsection we will deduce Theorem
1.12
from our results for the UIBOT.

We will only be working with longest directed paths in this subsection, so in what follows we assume that
\mathcal{X}
is the Busemann function in Theorem
1.4
with
{\operatorname{XDP}}=\operatorname{LDP}
.
The following deterministic lemma is the main input in the proof of the lower bound for LDP distances in
M_{0,n}
.

Figure 17:

Illustration of the proof of Lemma
7.1
. We prove a lower bound for the length of the segment of the leftmost LDP geodesic
P_{x_{-l}}
before it hits
z
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
l,k\in\mathbbm{N}
with
l<k
and recall that
\tau_{-k}
is the time from (
5.2
). There is a directed path contained in
M_{0,\tau_{-k}}
with length at least

\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{X}(-l)-\mathcal{X}(-k),0}}\right\}.

Proof.

This is an elementary geometric argument.
See Figure
17
for an illustration.
To lighten notation, we abbreviate
\operatorname{LDP}=\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}
.
Let
P_{x_{-l}}
and
P_{x_{-k}}
be the leftmost LDP geodesics in
M_{0,\infty}
from
x_{-l}
and
x_{-k}
to
\infty
, as in Lemma
5.4
.

Let
T
be the smallest time for which the edge
P_{x_{-l}}(T+1)
does not belong to
M_{0,\tau_{-k}}
and let
z
be the initial vertex of
P_{x_{-l}}(T+1)
. Then
P_{x_{-l}}|_{[0,T]}
is a directed path in
M_{0,\tau_{-k}}
from
x_{-l}
to
z
, of length
T=\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-l},z)
. We will prove a lower bound for this length.

Let
w
be any vertex with the property that the segments of
P_{x_{-l}}
and
P_{x_{-k}}
after they hit
w
are identical (such a vertex exists by Lemma
5.4
). By the definition of the Busemann function in Theorem
1.4
, we can choose
w
so that

\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-l},w)-\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-k},w)=\mathcal{X}(-l)-\mathcal{X}(-k).

(7.1)

The vertices
x_{-k}
and
z
each lie on the right boundary of
M_{\tau_{-k},\infty}
. The segment of this right boundary between
x_{-k}
and
z
is a directed path from
x_{-k}
to
z
in
M_{0,\infty}
. Therefore,

\operatorname{LDP}(z,w)\leq\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-k},w).

(7.2)

Since
z
and
w
are both hit by the LDP geodesic
P_{x_{-l}}
,

\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-l},w)=\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-l},z)+\operatorname{LDP}(z,w).

(7.3)

We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-l},z)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-l},w)-\operatorname{LDP}(z,w)\quad

by (
7.3
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}(-l)-\mathcal{X}(-k)+\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-k},w)-\operatorname{LDP}(z,w)\quad

by (
7.1
)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{X}(-l)-\mathcal{X}(-k)\quad

\displaystyle\text{by~\eqref{eqn-long-path-mono}}.

(7.4)

As we saw above, the directed path
P_{x_{-l}}|_{[0,T]}
is a directed path in
M_{0,\tau_{-k}}
with length
\operatorname{LDP}(x_{-l},z)
, so this concludes the proof.
∎

We can now prove the lower bound in Theorem
1.12
. We actually obtain a slightly more quantitative statement.

Lemma 7.2
.

For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
and
A>1
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{$M_{0,n}$ contains a directed path of length at least $A^{-1}n^{3/4}$}}}\right]\geq 1-A^{-2/3+o(1)},

(7.5)

where the
o(1)
goes to zero as
A\to\infty
, at a rate which is uniform in
n
.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
, which we will eventually send to zero to get the
o(1)
error in (
7.5
).
By Theorem
1.7
, the increments
\mathcal{X}(-k+1)-\mathcal{X}(-k)
for
k\geq 1
are i.i.d.
By Theorem
1.8
, there is a universal constant
c_{1}>0
such that for all
k\geq 1

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}(-k+1)-\mathcal{X}(-k)\geq A^{-1}n^{3/4}}}\right]\geq c_{1}A^{2/3}n^{-1/2}.

(7.6)

By multiplying the bound (
7.6
) over
\lfloor A^{-2/3+\varepsilon}n^{1/2}\rfloor
values of
k
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{X}(-k+1)-\mathcal{X}(-k):k\in[1,A^{-2/3+\varepsilon}n^{1/2}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}}\right\}\geq A^{-1}n^{3/4}}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq 1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-c_{1}A^{2/3}n^{-1/2}}}\right)^{\lfloor A^{-2/3+\varepsilon}n^{1/2}\rfloor}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq 1-c_{2}e^{-c_{3}A^{\varepsilon}}

(7.7)

for constants
c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
\varepsilon
.

The process
\mathcal{L}
is a random walk with i.i.d. increments sampled uniformly from
\{-1,0,1\}
. By (
5.3
), the time
\tau_{-k}
is the first positive time at which
\mathcal{L}
hits
-k
. By standard estimates for random walk,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{-\lfloor A^{-2/3+\varepsilon}n^{1/2}\rfloor}\leq n}}\right]\geq 1-c_{4}A^{-2/3+\varepsilon},

(7.8)

for a universal constant
c_{4}>0
.

By Lemma
7.1
, if the event in (
7.1
) occurs, then the map
M_{0,\tau_{-\lfloor A^{-2/3+\varepsilon}n^{1/2}\rfloor}}
contains a directed path of length at least
A^{-1}n^{3/4}
. If the event in (
7.8
) also occurs, then
M_{0,n}
also contains a directed path of length at least
A^{-1}n^{3/4}
. These events occur simultaneously with probability at least
1-O(A^{-2/3+\varepsilon})
. Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, this gives (
7.5
).
∎

We now turn our attention to the upper bound. The main input is the following elementary estimate for the maximum of the Busemann function.

Lemma 7.3
.

For each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
and
A>1
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max_{l\in[-k,k]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{X}(l)|\leq Ak^{3/2}}}\right]\geq 1-O(A^{-2/3}),

(7.9)

with the implicit constant in the
O(1)
uniform in
k
.

Proof.

By Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
, the increments
\mathcal{X}(l)-\mathcal{X}(l-1)
are independent and each
|\mathcal{X}(l)-\mathcal{X}(l-1)|
is stochastically dominated by a positive
2/3
-stable random variable (the scale parameter for this stable random variable can be taken to be uniform in
l
by the stationarity property in Theorem
1.7
).
Hence, we can couple
\mathcal{X}
with a collection
\{Y_{l}\}_{l\in\mathbbm{Z}}
of i.i.d. stable random variables in such a way that
|\mathcal{X}(l)-\mathcal{X}(l-1)|\leq Y_{l}
for each
l\in\mathbbm{Z}
. Under this coupling,

\max_{l\in[-k,k]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{X}(l)|\leq\sum_{l=-k+1}^{k}Y_{l},

which has the same law as
(2k)^{3/2}Y_{0}
by the stability property. The bound (
7.9
) then follows from the standard tail estimate for the stable distribution.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
and
A>1
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x,y):x,y\in\mathcal{V}(M_{0,n})}}\right\}\leq An^{3/4}}}\right]\geq 1-A^{-2/3+o(1)}

(7.10)

where the
o(1)
goes to zero as
A\to\infty
, uniformly in
n
, and we set
\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x,y)=-\infty
if there is no directed path in
M_{0,n}
from
x
to
y
.

Figure 18:

Illustration of the proofs of Lemmas
7.4
and
7.8
. The edge
\lambda_{0}
is part of
\beta_{0}^{R}
and the edge
\lambda_{n}
is part of
\beta_{n}^{R}
.

Proof.

Recall that
M_{n,\infty}
is the submap of
M_{-\infty,\infty}
obtained by applying the KMSW procedure to
(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+n)-\mathcal{Z}(n))|_{[0,\infty)}
.
Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be the Busemann function for
M_{n,\infty}
, defined in the same way as
\mathcal{X}
but with
M_{n,\infty}
in place of
M_{0,\infty}
. Since
M_{n,\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{0,\infty}
, we have
\mathcal{X}\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{X}_{n}
. The basic idea of the proof is as follows. By (
3.6
) of Lemma
3.6
, the number of edges on the boundary of
M_{0,n}
is bounded above by
2\max_{j\in[0,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{Z}(j)|+1
(with
|\mathcal{Z}(j)|
denoting the Euclidean norm), which is typically of order
n^{1/2}
.
Using Lemma
7.3
, we can therefore upper-bound the maximal values of each of
|\mathcal{X}|
and
|\mathcal{X}_{n}|
at the times corresponding to vertices on the boundary of
M_{0,n}
by
O(n^{3/4})
(see (
7.13
)). If there is a long directed path between two vertices of
M_{0,n}
, then by concatenating paths we would find that the values of
|\mathcal{X}_{n}|
on the upper boundary of
M_{0,n}
are much larger than the values of
|\mathcal{X}|
on the lower boundary of
M_{0,n}
. This would violate our bounds for
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
We now proceed with the details. See Figure
18
for an illustration.

Step 1: bounds for
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
Recall that
M_{0,n}=M_{0,\infty}\setminus M_{n+1,\infty}
.
Let
\beta_{0}^{R}
(resp.
\beta_{n}^{R}
) be the directed path which traverses the portion of the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
(resp.
M_{n,\infty}
) lying to the right of the initial vertex of
\lambda_{0}
(resp. initial vertex of
\lambda_{n}
). Let
\sigma_{0}^{R}
and
\sigma_{n}^{R}
be chosen so that
\beta_{0}^{R}|_{[0,\sigma_{0}^{R}]}
and
\beta_{n}^{R}|_{[0,\sigma_{n}^{R}]}
traverse the entirety of the upper-right and lower-right boundaries of
M_{0,n}
, respectively (recall Definition
3.3
). Then

\beta_{0}^{R}(k+\sigma_{0}^{R})=\beta_{n}^{R}(k+\sigma_{n}^{R}),\quad\forall k\geq 1.

(7.11)

In an exactly analogous manner, we also define the left boundary paths
\beta_{0}^{L}
and
\beta_{n}^{L}
and the times
\sigma_{0}^{L}
and
\sigma_{n}^{L}
, for which (
7.11
) holds with
L
in place of
R
.

By (
3.6
) of Lemma
3.6
,
\sigma_{0}^{R},\sigma_{n}^{R},\sigma_{0}^{L},\sigma_{n}^{L}
are each bounded above by
2\max_{j\in[0,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{Z}(j)|+1
.
By Hoeffding’s inequality for sums of i.i.d. bounded random variables, for any fixed
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sigma_{0}^{R},\sigma_{n}^{R},\sigma_{0}^{L},\sigma_{n}^{L}}}\right\}\leq A^{\varepsilon}n^{1/2}}}\right]\geq 1-c_{1}e^{-c_{2}A^{2\varepsilon}}

(7.12)

for universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
.

By (
7.12
) combined with Lemma
7.3
(applied once for each of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
and with
k=A^{\varepsilon}n^{1/2}
), it holds with probability at least
1-O(A^{-2(1-\varepsilon)/3})
that

\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\max_{l\in[-\sigma_{0}^{L},\sigma_{0}^{R}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{X}(l)|,\max_{l\in[-\sigma_{n}^{L},\sigma_{n}^{R}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{X}_{n}(l)|}}\right\}\leq An^{3/4}.

(7.13)

Step 2: relating
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
to longest directed paths.

Henceforth assume that the event in (
7.13
) occurs.
Let
\{x^{n}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
(resp.
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
) be the vertices on the boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
(resp.
M_{0,\infty}
), enumerated in west to east order so that
\lambda_{n}=(x_{0}^{n},x_{1}^{n})
(resp.
\lambda_{0}=(x_{0},x_{1})
). By (
7.11
) and its analog with
L
in place of
R
,

x_{k+\sigma_{0}^{R}}=x_{k+\sigma_{n}^{R}}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad x_{-k-\sigma_{0}^{L}}=x_{-k-\sigma_{n}^{L}}^{n},\quad\forall k\geq 0.

(7.14)

By the definition of the Busemann function in Theorem
1.4
, applied, e.g., to a sequence of points
\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbbm{N}}
on the right boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
, we can find a vertex
w
of
M_{0,\infty}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(l)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{l},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w),\quad\forall l\in[-\sigma_{0}^{L},\sigma_{0}^{R}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}(l)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{l}^{n},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},w),\quad\forall l\in[-\sigma_{n}^{L},\sigma_{n}^{R}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}.

(7.15)

We claim that every directed path in
M_{0,\infty}
started from
x_{\sigma_{n}^{R}}^{n}
must stay in
M_{n,\infty}
. Indeed, such a path cannot hit the boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
to the west of
x_{\sigma_{n}^{R}}^{n}
since this would create a directed cycle in
M_{-\infty,\infty}
. Such a path cannot cross the boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
to the east of
x_{\sigma_{n}^{R}}^{n}
since by the definition of
\sigma_{n}^{R}
, the portion of the boundary of
M_{n,\infty}
to the right of
x_{\sigma_{n}^{R}}^{n}
is also part of the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
. By (
7.14
),
x^{n}_{\sigma_{n}^{R}}=x_{\sigma_{0}^{R}}
. Hence

\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{\sigma_{n}^{R}}^{n},w)=\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{\sigma_{0}^{R}},w).

(7.16)

We thus obtain that with probability at least
1-O(A^{-2(1-\varepsilon)/3})

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w)}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{\sigma_{n}^{R}}^{n},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{\sigma_{0}^{R}},w)}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{X}_{n}(\sigma_{n}^{R})-\mathcal{X}(\sigma_{0}^{R})}}\right|\quad

by (
7.1
)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{X}_{n}(\sigma_{n}^{R})-\mathcal{X}(\sigma_{0}^{R})}}\right|\quad

by (
7.16
)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2An^{3/4}\quad

\displaystyle\text{by~\eqref{eqn-cell-bdy-sup}}.

(7.17)

Step 3: bound for paths in
M_{0,n}
.

Now let
x,y\in\mathcal{V}(M_{0,n})
be vertices which can be joined by a directed path in
M_{0,n}
. There is a vertex on
\partial M_{0,n}\cap\partial M_{0,\infty}
, i.e., a vertex
x_{l}
for
l\in[-\sigma_{0}^{L},\sigma_{0}^{R}]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
, which can be joined to
x
by a directed path in
M_{0,n}
. Indeed, it is enough to consider a segment of the upper-left boundary of
M_{0,j}
(whose edges are included in
M_{0,j}
), where
j\in[0,n]\cap\mathbbm{Z}
is chosen so that
x
is one of the vertices of
\lambda_{j}
.
Similarly, there exists
k\in[-\sigma_{n}^{L},\sigma_{n}^{R}]
such that
y
can be joined to
x_{k}^{n}
by a directed path in
M_{0,n}
. By concatenating paths from
x_{l}
to
x
to
y
to
x_{k}^{n}
to
w
, we get

\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{l},w)\geq\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x,y)+\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{k}^{n},w).

Re-arranging gives that with probability at least
1-O(A^{-2(1-\varepsilon)/3})

\displaystyle\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{l},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{k}^{n},w)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w)-\operatorname{LDP}_{M_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},w)+\mathcal{X}(l)-\mathcal{X}_{n}(k)\quad

by (
7.1
)

\displaystyle\leq 4An^{3/4}\quad

\displaystyle\text{by~\eqref{eqn-cell-bdy-sup} and~\eqref{eqn-ldp-upper-shift}}.

Since

\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, this gives (
7.10
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

Combine Lemmas
7.2
and
7.4
.
∎

7.2
SDP in size-
n
KMSW cells

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.13
.
Throughout, we assume that
\mathcal{X}
is the Busemann function of Theorem
1.4
with
{\operatorname{XDP}}=\operatorname{SDP}
.
We also let
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}
be the ordered sequence of vertices on the boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
, as in Theorem
1.4
, so that
x_{0}
is the initial vertex of the root edge.
We begin with a lower bound for shortest directed paths.

Lemma 7.5
.

Almost surely, for each
l,k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
l<k
,

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{l},x_{k})\geq\mathcal{X}(l)-\mathcal{X}(k).

(7.18)

Proof.

By the definition of the Busemann function (Theorem
1.4
), we can find
w\in M_{0,\infty}
(depending on
l
and
k
) such that

\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(l)=\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w)-\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{l},w).

(7.19)

Concatenating a directed path from
x_{l}
to
x_{k}
with a directed path from
x_{k}
to
w
gives a directed path from
x_{l}
to
w
, so

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{l},w)\leq\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w)+\operatorname{SDP}(x_{l},x_{k}).

(7.20)

(Note that there is at least one directed path from
x_{l}
to
x_{k}
, namely a segment of the right boundary of
M_{0,\infty}
.)
By re-arranging (
7.20
), then applying (
7.19
), we get (
7.18
).
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Recall the definition of the lower-right boundary of
M_{0,n}
from Definition
3.3
, and recall that
M_{0,n}
contains all of the edges on its lower-right boundary.
For each
\delta>0
, there exists
A=A(\delta)>1

such that for each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, the following is true. With probability at least
1-\delta
, there exists a vertex
y
on the lower-right boundary of
M_{0,n}
such that

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x_{0},y)\geq A^{-1}n^{3/8}.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.6
, the re-scaled Busemann function
k^{-3/4}\mathcal{X}(\lfloor k\cdot\rfloor)
restricted to positive times converges in the scaling limit when
k\to\infty
to a
4/3
-stable Lévy process with only positive jumps. Such a process has probability zero to stay positive during any positive-length time interval. From this, we get that there exists
A=A(\delta)>1
and
k_{*}=k_{*}(\delta)\in\mathbbm{N}
such that for each
k\geq k_{*}
, (the choice of
A^{-1/2}
is for later convenience)

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{$\exists r\in[0,k]\cap\mathbbm{Z}$ such that $\mathcal{X}(r)\leq-A^{-1/2}k^{3/4}$}}}\right]\geq 1-\delta/2.

(7.21)

The second coordinate
\mathcal{R}
of the KMSW encoding function is a random walk with i.i.d. increments sampled uniformly from
\{-1,0,1\}
.
By (
5.3
), when
k\geq 1
, the time
\tau_{k}
(which is the smallest time
j
that
x_{k}
is the initial vertex of
\lambda_{j}
) is the first positive time at which
\mathcal{R}
it hits
-k
. By standard estimates for random walks, there exists
A=A(\delta)>1
such that (
7.21
) holds and for each large enough
n\in\mathbbm{N}
(how large depends only on
\delta
),

\mathbbm{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{\lceil A^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\rceil}\leq n}}\right]\geq 1-\delta/2.

(7.22)

If
\tau_{\lceil A^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\rceil}\leq n
, then the vertices
x_{1},\dots,x_{\lceil A^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\rceil}
all lie on the lower-right boundary of
M_{0,n}
.
By (
7.21
) (with
k=\lceil A^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\rceil
) and (
7.22
), for each sufficiently large
n\in\mathbbm{N}
(how large depends only on
\delta
), the following is true.
It holds with probability at least
1-\delta
that there exists
r\in\mathbbm{N}
such that
x_{r}
lies on the lower-right boundary of
M_{0,n}
and
\mathcal{X}(r)\leq-A^{-1}n^{3/8}
. By Lemma
7.5
(applied with
(0,r)
in place of
(l,k)
), this implies that

\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},x_{r})\geq A^{-1}n^{3/8}.

(7.23)

Since
M_{0,n}\subset M_{0,\infty}
, we have
\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x_{0},x_{r})\geq\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},x_{r})
, which concludes the proof after enlarging the constant
A
so that the bound in (
7.23
) holds for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}
(this is always possible because
\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x_{0},x_{1})\geq 1
since
\lambda_{0}=(x_{0},x_{1})
is in
M_{0,n}
for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}
).
∎

Next we prove an upper bound for the SDP in
M_{0,n}
, which will be done via a similar argument as in the LDP case (Lemma
7.4
).

Lemma 7.7
.

For each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, it holds with probability tending to 1 as
A\to\infty
(uniformly in
k
) that

\max_{l\in[-k,k]\cap\mathbbm{Z}}|\mathcal{X}(l)|\leq Ak^{3/4}.

(7.24)

Proof.

This is immediate from the fact that
k^{-3/4}X(\lfloor k\cdot\rfloor)
converges in law to a stable Lévy process (Theorem
1.6
).
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, it holds with probability tending to 1 as
A\to\infty
(uniformly in
n
) that the following is true:

•

For every vertex
x
on the lower boundary of
M_{0,n}
, there exists a vertex
y
on the upper boundary of
M_{0,n}
such that
\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x,y)\leq An^{3/8}
.

•

For every vertex
y
on the upper boundary of
M_{0,n}
, there exists a vertex
x
on the lower boundary of
M_{0,n}
such that
\operatorname{SDP}_{M_{0,n}}(x,y)\leq An^{3/8}
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of Lemma
7.4
, except for some minor changes in Step 3. So, we will be brief. See again Figure
18
for an illustration.
Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be the Busemann function for
M_{n,\infty}
, defined in the same way as
\mathcal{X}
but with
M_{n,\infty}
in place of
M_{0,\infty}
. Since
M_{n,\infty}\overset{d}{=}M_{0,\infty}
, we have
